Part 2 looked at the invisible driving source through things of similar shape. So far, we have posed the question, seen the driving source as what I want to call Love, and looked for clues to it.

Part 3 looks at the inside of the person: how people receive the world and how they interpret it.

Part 3

How do people interpret the world?

About this part

Part 2 looked at the invisible driving source through similar shapes outside. Yet the driving source is also something felt inside the person. What is more, people sometimes mistake what they are driven by.

So Part 3 looks at the inside of the person: what people receive from the world, where it wells up, and where it spills out of words and numbers. What it borrows are the words of research on the body, the brain and cognition. There are four chapters.

  • "People cut out the world with signals of survival and trust": that the signals people pick up from the world as important have two directions, whether they can stay alive (survival) and whether they can stay connected (trust). It is the groundwork for how people receive trust, which Part 4 looks at alongside Love.
  • "Signals well up from inside the body": that those signals arrive from inside the body before they become words. It is for seeing why the driving source is hard to find on words and numbers.
  • "When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack": the feeling of lack when thoughts and the body's signals part. It is about how people sense something that is 0 when measured and yet is at work.
  • "Value is born in the consciousness of the one who receives": that value is born in the consciousness of the one who receives. It applies the shape of creation from Part 2 to the receiving side, and goes as far as the possibility that value and Love are similar in structure.
People cut out the world with signals of survival and trust

What do people receive from the world as important? Only the conditions for staying alive?

Typical conditions for staying alive are money and safety. pjdhiro talks about an example of their own like this.

pjdhiro's example (translated by Claude):I thought I was chasing money, but actually I was worried about my position among people, because losing would be miserable.

Money looks like a condition for staying alive. Yet what pjdhiro thought they were chasing and what they actually cared about were different. The signals people receive do not seem to be just one kind.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Probably humans have a system that processes something like signals of trust, and it is given weight. Separate from material survival. Enough that people can even take their own lives.

"What Is Consciousness" (in Japanese) holds that there are two directions in what people pick up from the world as important: whether they can stay alive (survival), and whether they can stay connected (trust). The two work at the same time and enter into each other. The system separate from material survival in pjdhiro's view corresponds to this direction of trust. Part 4 looks at this direction of trust as something similar to the feeling called Love.

Symbols such as words and numbers are lined up in a single row, so when things are received as symbols, the value for survival may be known while the value for trust remains unknown. The source of the sense of something being off that arises then cannot be found on the symbols.

What Is Consciousness (in Japanese)What do people cut out the world with? From the signals of survival and trust, looking at the vortex given the name consciousness.uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/as
More — why trust is placed separately from survival

"What Is Consciousness" looks, in the direction of survival, at threat, safety, status and resources, and in the direction of trust, at attachment, belonging, trust and intersubjectivity (experience and meaning being shared between people). Why is trust placed separately from survival? pjdhiro had knowledge in the head from books on psychoanalysis, developmental psychology, neurotransmitters and neuroeconomics (Psychology that influenced pjdhiro, in Japanese). Within that, pjdhiro had the following experience.

pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):At the last stage of metacognition, when, like an ouroboros, the seeing I and the seen I are born, there is no sense of fit there; rather, separate from the seeing I, there is a feeling as if there is something, a presence, at the end of a cable like an umbilical cord beyond the top of my head, that gives a stability like the universe.

This stability looks like something other than what is gained by securing the conditions for staying alive. pjdhiro sees it as possible that people cut out the longing for the presence that gives this sense of reassurance, reading it as trust (pjdhiro's own words are in "A measure that tells of a gap" in the chapter "What aspects does what I want to call Love have?").

"Signals of survival and trust" is a name for convenience. Strictly, pjdhiro sees them as perhaps the survival signals of fight and flight, and mental signals of pleasure and displeasure such as trust and aversion. On the research side, no division along exactly these two lines can be found, and the number of signals is not settled at one either. Research measuring the signals that work between people (intersubjectivity) is growing, but how to read it is not settled. What was researched is in the fold "Why place trust separately from survival" in "What Is Consciousness" and in its research records (in Japanese).

More — with a single row of symbols, the value of trust cannot be seen

Symbols such as words and numbers are lined up in a single row. In pjdhiro's estimation, people anticipate (predict) two values, survival and trust. When what is received is a single row of symbols, things like "agreement on the value concerning survival, but the value on the trust side unknown" happen.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):…A sense that something is off arises: that excessively chasing desires related to survival might damage trust.

The source of that sense cannot be found on the symbols. Even so, one can hold that sense without closing it, keeping it open.

More — the two are mistaken for each other and enter into each other

As in the example at the head of the chapter, of thinking one was chasing money while actually caring about one's position among people, the two, survival and trust, are not always told apart within a person. Struggling by trying to solve a problem on the side of trust with achievement on the side of survival is also one of the situations in which the two are mistaken for each other. In business, the question of profit and the long term in the value creation section of the chapter "Where are the words of business misunderstood?" is an example.

There is always somewhere perception does not reach

The range of visible light and the breadth of ways of seeing both have limits, and there is always somewhere perception does not reach. Mass not visible in light was inferred from how stars on the outskirts of galaxies rotate. Where symbols show 0 might be likened to this mass not visible in light. Even unseen, the body may be receiving something.

Signals well up from inside the body

The previous chapter saw that a sense of something being off wells up whose source cannot be seen on the symbols. Where does that sense come from?

When irritation or anxiety wells up, people look for the person or event that caused it. In the evening, one may be badly irritated by a remark from a family member, snap back, and then, after eating something, find the irritation has quietly gone. The remark has not changed at all. The source of the irritation seems to have been hunger, a signal from inside the body. The body's signal wells up first, and whom it is about and what kind of feeling it is are read afterwards.

As something pjdhiro wants to check through neurophenomenology, pjdhiro poses the following question about how those signals are tied between the body and consciousness. "Objects of metacognition" and "holding" in the question are words used as the axes that divide stages on the prerequisite page "What are the stages of mental development" (in Japanese): what one can see as an object, and how much one can hold without dividing it off.

pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):What are the signals that tie the body and consciousness, with respect to the objects of metacognition and to holding them? This is very important, tangled up with the mystery of where mind and body connect.

"What Is Consciousness" (in Japanese) places the source of the signals in the sense by which the body receives its own internal state. This sense, receiving heartbeat, breathing and the condition of the internal organs, is called interoception. Some research holds that the brain predicts the state of the body ahead of time, moment by moment, and corrects the prediction with the signals that arrive; in this view, what stands out as a signal is the gap from the prediction. The sense of the inside of the body arrives, before becoming words, as a vague feeling, roughly whether it is pleasant or unpleasant, calm or aroused. This may be why the source of the sense of something being off cannot be found on the symbols. It may also be for the same reason that what I want to call Love is hard to find when searched for on words and numbers.

More — signals stand out as gaps from prediction

This question has just begun to be researched in "What Is Consciousness", setting it against research in neurophenomenology.

The body is always receiving its internal state, such as heartbeat, breathing and the condition of the internal organs. This sense is called interoception and is defined as the sense of the physiological condition of the body (Seth 2013, who cites it as Craig's 2002 definition). "What Is Consciousness" places the source of the signals here. It is the view that consciousness does not start from abstract thought but rises on top of the state of the body.

Some research holds that much of the sense of the inside of the body is made of prediction (Barrett and Simmons 2015, Seth 2013). The brain predicts the state of the body ahead of time, moment by moment, and corrects that prediction with the signals arriving from the body. In this view, what stands out as a signal is the gap between the prediction and what was received.

Not every gap rises into consciousness. The research writes that gaps from prediction may also be ignored (Barrett and Simmons 2015). "What Is Consciousness" also holds that many gaps are absorbed without being noticed, and only some are sent further on.

It is also written that the sense of the inside of the body is usually experienced as a rough feeling, roughly whether it is pleasant or unpleasant, calm or aroused (Barrett 2017). That is, it arrives as a vague feeling before becoming words. The same research holds that the same inner bodily sensation can have many causes, and that the brain makes emotions by sorting out sensations case by case with the concepts it has learned (Barrett 2017). The example at the head of the chapter, in which the discomfort of hunger is felt as irritation at a family member, is close to this view.

The gap from prediction and the lack people feel differ both in the level at which they are described and in timescale. The gap from prediction is something measured from outside, a matter of the brain's workings moment by moment. Lack is the experience of a person feeling from inside that something is missing, a matter of times long enough for the person to notice. pjdhiro's estimation in the previous chapter (that people anticipate two values, survival and trust) is also a matter of times long enough to notice. It has the same shape of "anticipating ahead", but how the two connect is not yet known.

More — what is measured and what is felt can disagree

Even meditators with long training in turning attention to the inside of the body did no better than untrained people on a task of sensing their own heartbeat. Even so, the meditators rated their own performance higher and found the task easier than untrained people did (Khalsa et al. 2008). For the sense of the inside of the body, some research separates the accuracy of sensing, one's rating of one's own sensing, and awareness of whether that rating is accurate as different things (Garfinkel et al. 2015).

What is felt in the first person and what is measured from outside do not always overlap. A way of researching that sets the two against each other has just begun in "What Is Consciousness".

(2015) "Knowing your own heart", Biological Psychology 104:65 (full text checked against the PDF of the journal pages in the University of Sussex institutional repository (published under CC BY-NC-ND 3.0); the distinction of three dimensions in the abstract on p.65).

When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack

People sometimes feel from inside that something is missing or does not fit. There is an example in which the body received a lack first, while it could not yet be put into words.

In the 1980s, Klein and colleagues interviewed fireground commanders about how they had made their decisions. One lieutenant, on the ground floor of a house, noticed that water on the visible flames did not work as expected, and that the room was unusually hot and unusually quiet. Without knowing what was happening, the lieutenant ordered the crew out of the house. Within a minute of their getting out, the floor collapsed. The seat of the fire was in the basement, where the lieutenant had not expected it. The lieutenant described the decision as coming from a "sixth sense". There was no fire in the basement in the lieutenant's thinking. Even so, the body was receiving that something did not fit.

How does pjdhiro feel that lack in the body?

pjdhiro's image (translated by Claude):If I were to liken the bodily sense I call kesson-driven thinking, it is an intuition that has a value on the imaginary axis and yet is 0 in cognition.

The imaginary axis is an axis standing at right angles to the original number line, and its value, measured on the original axis, comes out as 0. It is like how, measuring height from above a map, even the highest mountain comes out as 0. Measured with words and numbers it is 0, and yet the body is receiving something: that is the shape of this image.

The map of the house in the lieutenant's head had no basement floor. Comparing flames, water and heat on that map, the fire in the basement appears nowhere. On the map it is 0. Even so, the heat and the quiet were arriving from a direction not on the map. This is not to say that bodily feelings are always right. What is seen here is the shape in which what was received had no place on the map of thought.

pjdhiro sees that when thoughts and the mind part from the signals welling up in the body, people tend to feel that something is lacking. "Kesson-Driven Thinking" (in Japanese) calls this lack kesson (a lack or deficit). It does not fix kesson in one sentence, but lines up pjdhiro's ways of putting it as aspects of kesson. The image above is one of them; others include "Among the things perceived on the real axis, something that was defined as zero because it was zero and treated as meaningless or valueless actually had meaning on the imaginary axis. I think something like that is kesson" and "Feeling a sense of something being off, or of lack. Error is one of those." (translated by Claude). Kesson is also something noticed through error against prediction.

An error is a subtraction: taking the difference between the expected value and the value that occurred. While looking with Claude for how to put kesson-driven thinking, the conversation once stopped going anywhere. pjdhiro later looked back on where it had got stuck like this.

pjdhiro's reflection (translated by Claude):I was doing subtraction. But it didn't feel right. It wasn't a one-dimensional number line, but Claude interpreted it that way and the conversation stopped going anywhere.

Subtracting on a one-dimensional number line, the value on an axis standing at right angles does not show up in the difference. Subtraction outside the number line is laid out in the chapter of Kesson-Driven Thinking "“I was doing subtraction. Outside the number line.”" (in Japanese).

A lack may be felt when the anticipation of the signals of survival and trust and the experience part. It may be that, because what I want to call Love is driving, people cannot help passively feeling this lack.

Kesson-Driven Thinking (in Japanese)A way of thinking that treats a sense of something being off, undefined requirements and weak signals as questions.uminomae.github.io/project-design-theory/ks
More — vision and planning start from a lack

The lack eventually becomes a problem to be solved. pjdhiro sees vision, design and planning as starting from here, and the problem solving beyond it as the range management studies has dealt with so far.

Kesson-driven thinking is a stance of thinking and mind that takes the feeling of lack, or a sense of something being off, as its driving source. Its range, structure and process are still being explored.

Kesson-driven thinking can also be called an attitude of picking up error as a question, or a feeling that there is something in a direction other than the aspect being seen. This may be one of the ways of thinking that Love-Driven Development needs. There may also be other means of touching what people cannot perceive.

"Kesson-Driven Thinking" calls the function of holding an error as a question, rather than processing it reflexively, hōji (holding), and values holding on to it as a question without answering right away when one notices a kesson. Holding a sense of something being off without resolving it right away may make judgment bound to a single aspect less likely.

pjdhiro also sees that holding needs a supporting environment and relationships, and becomes difficult in states of strong anxiety or at earlier stages (on stages, see the chapter "What looks threatening changes with the stage of development").

More — the imaginary axis, and the axis of being

Why the imaginary axis stands at right angles to the original number line is on the prerequisite page "The multiplication of rotation" (in Japanese). The value on an axis standing at right angles comes out as 0 when measured on the original axis.

pjdhiro sees it as possible that an axis of being stands at right angles to the axis of the action of "thinking and deciding". Symbols such as words and numbers are things lined up on the axis of "thinking and deciding". When the column in the first chapter of this part, "People cut out the world with signals of survival and trust", wrote that even where symbols show 0 the body is receiving something, this is the 0 it meant.

People sometimes feel this magnitude, which does not show up in numbers, as something important. pjdhiro sees the value on this imaginary axis as also being value for humankind. Here it connects with what management studies has called "value creation".

More — why something is felt where it is 0

The stability of "one cannot see ahead, and yet one is stable", touched on in Seeing the invisible center by dividing oneself in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?", may also be a magnitude received at this 0.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):As for why one can observe the imaginary axis in zero: one answer is that I do feel the stability of the umbilical cord, and that may be all of it. That is why I was following the signals of survival and trust in "What Is Consciousness". I think wondering what it is, if that signal wells up at a place that is zero, is the noticing of kesson.

Sources

On the lieutenant's example at the head of the chapter, the report reads the cue as a departure from the pattern in which heat, sound and water usually go together.

Sources: Klein, Calderwood & Clinton-Cirocco (1988) Rapid Decision Making on the Fire Ground, Technical Report 796, U.S. Army Research Institute (full text checked against the record of the DTIC PDF in the Internet Archive's Wayback Machine (16 February 2024); the order to leave the house, the floor collapsing, the fire being in the basement, the "sixth sense", and the reading that a departure from the pattern of heat, sound and water was the cue on p.13; the interview summary in Appendix B, Incident Summary #4, p.B-8).

Value is born in the consciousness of the one who receives

pjdhiro thought that this exploration could at least answer "what is value creation". Then where is value born?

The first answer that comes to mind is: inside the product. It is usual to think that makers put value into a product. Something hard to explain with that idea happens when listening to a song. A song one has heard many times may suddenly pierce the heart when heard after a heartbreak. Not one note of the song has changed. What changed is what wells up inside the listener.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Value is born in the customer's consciousness. Through the signals at the stage before it.

Value is a vague word in which things of different natures are mixed in one term. Let us apply the process of creation set out in "What Is Creation", field → wave → edge → vortex → bundle, to the receiving side, and follow it with the song at the head of the chapter. The song, at the stage of the edge where it meets the listener, becomes a trigger for signals to well up. What pierces the heart is the stage of the vortex, where consciousness receives the many signals that welled up as one important whole: the state of feeling value. The reputation "a great song" and the number sold are the bundle that has become words and numbers. The song did not change; the listener's field changed with the heartbreak. From meeting the same song, a different vortex from before has formed. Seen this way, the maker putting something in and the listener feeling it in their consciousness can be read as one continuous movement. When people usually say "value", the trigger at the edge, the state of the vortex, and the bundle that has become words, evaluations and prices overlap in one word, making it hard to see what is happening where.

Value, too, may be similar in structure to what I want to call Love: a bundle of signals hard to put into words.

More — five stages on the receiving side

Makers putting value into a product also really happens. To see how putting it in connects with what is felt in the customer's consciousness, let us divide the word "value". Applying to the receiving side the process of creation set out by "What Is Creation", field → wave → edge → vortex → bundle, seen in the fold "Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves" in the chapter "What shape does creation take?", it can be divided, for example, as follows.

  • Field: the situation the customer lives in, their relationships, what they know, their values. It also includes broad things such as the world, the era and culture.
  • Wave: differences arise within the field and split into customer and product. Like demand and supply, the side that seeks and the side that offers face each other.
  • Edge: where customer and product meet. When they meet, the signals of the stage before well up in the customer. Products, services and what the maker put in become triggers for feeling value here.
  • Vortex: where consciousness receives the many signals that welled up as one important whole. It is the state of feeling value.
  • Bundle: what has become words, evaluations and prices and is shared among people. It is the expression of value.

How it is felt changes with the field the customer lives in.

More — the five stages, from the chapters so far

What customers know, and what they feel to be a matter of course, grows within the field. What society recognizes also changes the value felt in the same product (Making money, and being able to recognize value).

The wave is splitting. With differences arising within the same field, customer and product face each other as the side that seeks and the side that offers.

The signals that well up at the edge are the signals of the two directions set out by "What Is Consciousness", seen in the chapter People cut out the world with signals of survival and trust. They well up in the layer of the body, and consciousness receives them.

As for the vortex, value, including the value on the imaginary axis seen in the fold of the chapter "When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack", has many axes, and may be a synthesis of as many axes as there are adjectives.

Words and prices that have become a bundle become part of the field again, and enter into the next meeting and into how makers put things in.

The five stages do not necessarily proceed in order in one pass. They run at the same time and enter into one another. If the maker's side also has the same five stages, it may also be possible to see it as two vortices meeting at an edge.

Part 3 looked at how people interpret the world. So far, we have posed the question, seen the driving source as what I want to call Love, and looked for clues to it in similar shapes and in how people receive the world.

In Part 4, before going on to what I want to call Love, two questions are answered: what is trust, and what is reassurance. pjdhiro sees trust and reassurance as also involved in the definition of Love. The part sets out premises of the mind, looks at the differences from similar things from several viewpoints of psychological theory, lines up outlines, and looks at them again through stages and personality.

Part 4

How does the mind feel trust and reassurance?

About this part

In the chapter at the end of Part 1, "What has the word Love pointed to?", what has been called Love was lined up. Looking at it, pjdhiro sees it this way.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I think the feeling called Love resembles feelings like trust and reassurance.

On the side of research, too, Love has been written about overlapping with what are called trust and reassurance, sometimes mixed into a single word. In the classification of emotions, something close to trust is placed right next to love; in attachment research, the bond that has been called love is written in the word attachment, and that attachment is said to come, in humans, with calm and security. Whether they look overlapping because they are similar, or because they are received mixed together, is not yet known. In this part, the side of trust and reassurance is divided finely and looked at from the viewpoints of psychology.

More — research that has written about Love overlapping with trust and reassurance

In the classification of emotions, Plutchik places love as a mixture of joy and acceptance, and lists "trusting" as a personality word derived from acceptance (2001). In attachment research, Bowlby writes the child's bond to the mother, in his own theory, in the words tie and attachment behaviour, and uses the word love where he introduces the theories of Freud and others (1958). Fisher counts attachment as one of the emotion systems involved in mating and reproduction, and writes that in humans it comes with feelings of calm and security (1998). In the brains of people who said they were still intensely in love after long years of marriage, regions found in research on maternal attachment were active along with the same reward regions as in new love (Acevedo et al., 2012). Fredrickson writes that what people point to when they say "I love you" largely includes feelings, bonds, closeness and commitment accumulated over time (2016).

Each can be read as an example of what is called trust or reassurance entering into what is called Love.

First, five premises of the mind are set out (the unconscious, the sources of the feelings of trust and love, defense, the situations in which defense loosens, and the stages of development). Next, trust and reassurance are looked at through their differences from similar things. The similar things are grouped under four viewpoints of psychological theory (self-actualization, psychological defense mechanisms, theories of the mother–child relationship, and the classification of emotions), so this part can also be read as a guide to theories of psychology. Last, the outlines that appear are lined up and looked at again through stages and personality. The five chapters of premises set out an overview of the theories, each one entered through an example. Readers who know the theories of psychology can go on to the chapter "Why are feelings similar to trust hard to tell apart?". Readers who do not can follow the "Sources" at the end of each chapter to the original sources and, where one exists, to a Japanese translation.

What is the unconscious?

Is the human mind built the way the person thinks it is? The most important scene of the novel Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde was not something its author, Stevenson, thought up while awake. Stevenson struggled for two days without finding a plot. On the second night, in a dream he had while asleep, there was a scene in which Hyde takes a drug and changes his form in front of his pursuers. After waking, Stevenson thought up and wrote the rest himself, while awake. Later, in the essay "A Chapter on Dreams" (1888), Stevenson called those who make up stories and show them to him in his dreams the "Little People", and asked who they are and who it is that dreams. Stevenson answers that the one who dreams is himself. Yet he also writes that the ways of the Little People can be seen even in the parts he thought he had written himself while awake.

pjdhiro sees the human mind this way.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):The human psyche is not built the way we think it is. Freud's transference; sources bearing on the definitions of trust and love, like Klein's object relations; Jung's archetypes; irrational behavior and the arising of feelings through psychological defense mechanisms, stages of development and states — none of us is built as admirably as we think.

In a 1915 paper Freud wrote that, just as with physical things, the mind is not necessarily built the way it appears to us. Psychoanalysis has called the side of the mind's workings that moves without rising into consciousness the unconscious. It includes both what simply happens not to be in consciousness at the moment and what has been kept from rising into consciousness (the repressed). In a 1917 paper Jung divided the unconscious into the personal unconscious, which comes from the individual's memories, and the collective unconscious, common to all people. His clue was that fantasies with no root in the individual's memory appear in the same form as the themes of myths and legends all over the world; Jung sees the images that appear in the deep layer of the unconscious and the themes of myths as images of the same form. Jung also writes that humankind has drawn gods and devils from such primordial images. Hayao Kawai's readings of myths and folk tales are placed on the separate page "Psychology that influenced pjdhiro" (in Japanese; under construction).

When thinking, in Kesson-Driven Thinking, about what gets set to zero without being noticed, pjdhiro answered as follows.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Besides not being able to perceive it for bodily reasons, there is also behavior as if one does not want to perceive it for mental reasons, which is why it gets set to zero.

What Freud calls the repressed is what has been kept from rising into consciousness, and this page reads it as overlapping with the side of what one does not want to perceive. What cannot be perceived for bodily reasons lies on a different side from the workings of the mind Freud dealt with in this paper.

Do people touch, in daily life, the side that moves without rising into consciousness? pjdhiro talks about dreams this way.

pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):I make a habit of thinking back over my sleep dreams. When I wake up. I feel it has an effect like the point of keeping a diary, but above all, it makes it easier to accept that there clearly are things I cannot control. The me that I think of is narrow. Like that.

In The Interpretation of Dreams (1900), Freud took his own dreams as his main material and interpreted them through self-analysis. Of the first dream he interprets in the book, Freud writes that the meaning of the dream came into view as he interpreted it, and sums up that the content of the dream was the fulfillment of a wish, and its motive was a wish. In a lecture he prepared in 1914, Jung begins by describing the dream as something that seems to happen by chance, from outside the stream of consciousness. He then saw a compensating work of the unconscious toward consciousness in the way thoughts and tendencies little acknowledged in everyday consciousness start to move of themselves during sleep. Jung also writes that people can be influenced by a dream even without understanding it with the head.

How have dreams been studied?

Dreams have been examined both within psychoanalysis and in research on the sleeping brain. Here, three are lined up.

Dreams and myths. In his 1917 paper, Jung writes that it is not suitable to read images coming from the deep layer of the unconscious by pulling them back to personal matters, and gives as an example the dream of one patient. The patient dreamed that, when trying to cross a wide, fast-flowing river by finding a shallow place, a large crab hidden in the water seized the patient's foot, and woke up in fear. Jung read the crab as a living content of the unconscious that analysis cannot exhaust, and wrote that it closely resembles the myths of heroes fighting monsters at the water's edge. In the same passage Jung calls myths the dreams of peoples.

The dreaming brain. Later, dreams also came to be studied by measuring the brains of sleeping people. Dreams have long been thought of as tied to REM sleep (the period in sleep when the eyes move rapidly), but they are also seen in non-REM sleep (Siclari et al., 2017). Siclari and colleagues woke sleeping people several times during the night and asked whether they had been dreaming, had experienced something but could not recall its content, or had experienced nothing. In both kinds of sleep, when people answered that they had been dreaming, slow-wave activity was reduced in a region toward the back of the brain. During non-REM sleep, by watching the activity of this region, it was possible to predict, before waking them, whether the person would answer that they had been dreaming.

Talking with people inside a dream. Dreams told after waking are easily distorted or forgotten, and that has been a difficulty of dream research (Konkoly et al., 2021). Konkoly and colleagues, across four laboratories, had people who were aware within the dream that they were dreaming (people having lucid dreams) hear arithmetic questions while still asleep, and answer with eye movements or movements of the facial muscles. Of 36 people, 6 answered correctly, 29 times in all. Of the attempts at asking questions, a correct answer came back in 18.4%.

Where do the feelings of trust and love come from?

People come to trust someone, or to like someone. Is that feeling born each time from looking at the person in front of them?

The composer Berlioz saw Harriet Smithson play Ophelia in Hamlet, performed by an English company that had come to Paris. The heading of that chapter of his memoirs speaks of a deadly love. Berlioz writes that what her acting did to his imagination and heart could be compared only to the shock he received from Shakespeare. At Romeo and Juliet the next day, in the third act, he says he thought that he was lost. Berlioz also writes that at the time he did not know a single word of English, and saw Shakespeare only through the fog of French translation. Before he had ever spoken with Smithson, and while he could not understand the words on the stage, the love had begun. The memoirs write that Smithson became his wife five years later.

pjdhiro's words in the previous chapter named, as sources bearing on the definitions of trust and love, Freud's transference, Klein's object relations and Jung's archetypes. Of the psychology that influenced pjdhiro, pjdhiro names Freud's transference first.

pjdhiro's impression (translated by Claude):The phenomenon of transference became a starting point for me to think, from experience, about emotional contagion in general.

Psychoanalysis has written that some of the feelings of trust and love come not only from the person in front of one, but also from patterns already within oneself. In 1912 Freud wrote that, from what they are born with and the influences of childhood, people acquire a pattern of under what conditions and how they love, and repeat it throughout life. Part of the pattern remains unknown to the person. When the need for love is not fully satisfied in reality, that pattern is turned toward new people one meets. Freud called its being turned toward the doctor in the setting of treatment transference. In the same paper Freud writes that emotional ties such as sympathy (Sympathie), friendship and trust, however pure they feel to the person, lead back, when traced, to sexual drives.

In 1946 Klein wrote that the infant receives its first object split into a good one that satisfies and a bad one that does not, and that this becomes the dividing line between love and hate. In 1917 Jung wrote that within transference, primordial images common to people, which cannot be explained by the person's memories, are also projected (the chapter "What is the unconscious?"). In the same paper Jung writes that when such images are projected onto people around one, they become excessive exaltation or contempt, producing infatuation and conflict. In a 1919 paper Jung calls inherited patterns of the mind by the word archetypes.

What the three have in common is the view that some of the feelings of trust and love come not only from the person in front of one, but from patterns one is not conscious of. The patterns the three describe differ: a version of childhood relationships (Freud), images split into good and bad (Klein), images common to people (Jung). Rather than deciding which is right, one can also see them, like the blind men and the elephant, as touching different aspects of the same thing.

Dora Hecht), Collected Papers on Analytical Psychology, 2nd ed., London: Baillière, Tindall and Cox, 1920, pp.354–444 (full text checked, in translation; substitute for parental authority p.408, the father imago p.409, primordial images p.410, exaltation and contempt toward people around one pp.432–433; the copy is the Universal Library edition on the Internet Archive; the translator's name was also checked in a copy of the 1922 printing) / Jung (1919) "Instinct and the Unconscious", British Journal of Psychology 10(1):15–23, doi:10.1111/j.2044-8295.1919.tb00003.x (full text checked; archetypes and the collective unconscious p.19, the word taken from Augustine p.21; the copy is a public-domain version on Zenodo; the link is being re-checked, so for now only the publisher's DOI link is given).

Klein's paper is in Merani Kurain chosakushū 4: Mōsōteki/bunretsuteki sekai (Seishin Shobō, 1985) under the title "Bunretsuteki kisei ni tsuite no oboegaki" (CiNii Books).

The mind has mechanisms to protect itself

After an expectation is betrayed, people despair, blame others or accept (we will see this in pjdhiro's words in the later chapter "How do expectation and trust differ?"). What decides which movement comes out?

An excuse is thought of as something a person says knowingly. The fox's excuse in Aesop's fable "The Fox and the Grapes", on the other hand, is not meant for anyone to hear. A hungry fox tries to take a bunch of grapes hanging from a trellis, but cannot reach it however hard it tries. As it leaves, the fox says to itself, "They are not ripe yet." An old English translation renders this scene as the fox "beguiling herself of her disappointment". The fable's closing line says that among people, too, there are those who, when they cannot accomplish something because their own strength falls short, blame the circumstances. As the Japanese saying "sour grapes" (as in English) shows, it is a movement everyone knows from experience.

pjdhiro wrote about the relation between how such movements come out and personality in the entry "Personality" of a page of pjdhiro's own terms, as follows.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Personality can also be called the strength and tendencies of psychological defense. People have psychological patterns for a given moment (like methods or functions in an emotion-processing library), and use them constantly. Because the same pattern of processing seems to run in similar situations, it becomes the bias called personality.

Psychoanalysis has called the mind's moves to protect itself from anxiety and pain defense mechanisms (each defense mechanism is gathered on the separate page "What are psychological defense mechanisms?", in Japanese; under construction). Vaillant renamed these moves involuntary coping mechanisms and wrote that, like fever, they are not a sign of illness but a coping response. Which move comes out cannot be chosen, and is not visible to the person using it. Vaillant arranged defenses into levels and also placed that arrangement on a continuum of the maturity of personality. The difference between despairing, blaming others and accepting after an expectation is betrayed can also be read as a difference in how defense comes out. The fox that called the grapes sour can also be read not as having chosen an excuse, but as having been protected by such a move from the disappointment of not reaching them.

What psychology has said about personality (the view that sorts into types and the view that measures traits, and how the classifications of personality disorders and the Enneagram resemble each other) is gathered on the separate page "What is personality?" (in Japanese; under construction).

Sources

Sources: pjdhiro's view of personality at the head of the chapter = the entry "Personality" in pjdhiro's "Terms: other" (uminomae.github.io/pjdhiro/word-other/, in Japanese).

Sources: for the fable at the head of the chapter: Aesop, "The Fox and the Grapes (Ἀλώπηξ καὶ βότρυς)", in Chambry (ed.) Ésope, Fables, Paris: Les Belles Lettres, 1927, fable 32 (full text checked; the hungry fox, not reaching, saying to itself as it leaves "they are not ripe (ὄμφακές εἰσιν)", and the closing line that people who cannot accomplish things for lack of strength blame the times, pp.17–18; the copy is the Greek Wikisource) / English translation: Townsend, Three Hundred Æsop's Fables, London: George Routledge and Sons, 1887, p.128 (full text checked, in translation; "beguiling herself of her disappointment"; the copy is the English Wikisource).

Sources: Vaillant (2011) "Involuntary coping mechanisms: a psychodynamic perspective", Dialogues in Clinical Neuroscience 13(3):366 (full text checked; full text at PMC3182012; involuntary coping, the fever analogy, the grandchild example and Freud 1894 in the opening section "What are involuntary coping mechanisms?"; the six properties, four levels, three routes and the Study of Adult Development in the section "Classification of defenses"; pp.366–370).

To read the originals: Vaillant's paper can be read in full on PMC. The classification of defenses is in the section "Classification of defenses".

In what situations does the mind's defense loosen?

The previous chapter saw that the mind has moves to protect itself from anxiety and pain, and that those moves are not visible to the person using them. Then when does that protection loosen?

Faced with a stubborn person, we tend to think that the protection is that person's personality, and that the only way is to break it down with well-reasoned argument. There is research that checked, in a word, that the sun works better than the north wind. In 1993 the psychologist Miller and colleagues, when giving people with drinking problems the results of an assessment of their drinking, assigned them by lot to a style that argues the need to change and confronts resistance, and a style that listens and reflects back the person's own views. In the group with the listening style, resistance was about half. Miller writes that how much resistance comes out depends far more on the counselor's style than on the person's personality. Yet in both groups, the results of the assessment were given back and reality was shown. What differed was the way it was given back.

At the core of the listening style is the way of listening that Carl Rogers, of client-centered therapy, called empathy: listening carefully to what the other says and giving it back slightly rephrased (active listening). Miller writes that this way of giving back is less likely to call up resistance. The six conditions Rogers named are on the separate page "What is anxiety?" (in Japanese).

As examples of things that loosen the protection, that enfold, pjdhiro names empathy, holding, reverie and not-knowing. On that basis, pjdhiro sees what is important for mental growth like this.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I think two things are important for mental growth: active things that point at the mind, like cognitive behavioral therapy, shikan or metacognition; and being in situations that undo defense, like empathy and relationships of trust. So much so that I think those two alone are enough. Separation and inclusion. That is, poking at defense and pulling one back to reality, and making a safe situation where defense is not needed. I think that, often, I feel that the balance spoken of in child-rearing and education is about this.

The first is the side of watching one's own mind as an object, the side that points. Watching emotions as objects appears as emotional metacognition in the chapter "Why are feelings similar to trust hard to tell apart?". Cognitive behavioral therapy and shikan are examples of this side.

The second is the enfolding side, of situations that undo protection. Winnicott called not only physically holding the infant but the whole of the provision surrounding the infant "holding", and wrote that it is reliable, not mechanically, but in a way that includes empathy for the other (the chapter "How do reassurance and dependence differ from trust?"). An introduction to Winnicott's theory is on the separate page "Psychology that influenced pjdhiro" (in Japanese). Empathic understanding and reverie (Bion's containing) are also examples of this side. In Miller's interviews at the head of the chapter, the first side, giving back the results, and the second side, listening and reflecting back, were in the same setting.

🚧 Under construction: cognitive behavioral therapy, shikan, empathic understanding and reverie will be written after reading and checking the full text of the sources.

Of the examples on the enfolding side, pjdhiro says this about what not-knowing points to.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I think it was the Jungians such as Hayao Kawai: when one comes to have the same experience as the client, like empathy or countertransference, the subjective state at that time is re-experiencing, just as it is, the sense of "not knowing" the conflicts and problems the client carries. I think that's what it said. It's that state of being troubled, lost and at a loss. Not knowing what to do. Not knowing what is wrong either. Not knowing the problem or the solution. That kind of not-knowing.

🚧 Under construction: the theories of Hayao Kawai and the Jungians will be written after reading and checking the full text of the sources.
