To read the originals: a Japanese translation of Winnicott's paper is in Jōcho hattatsu no seishin bunseki riron (tr. Sadanobu Ushijima, Iwasaki Gakujutsu Shuppansha, 1977) (CiNii Books). Maslow's paper can be read in full in York University's Classics in the History of Psychology. The safety needs are pp.376–380.

How do attachment and trust differ?

It was also said that holding a sense of something being off without resolving it right away (holding) is hard to sustain alone, and is supported by stable relationships. Then why can one keep holding it even when there is no one nearby?

When one feels helpless, someone who is beside one and holds one's hand is a support. Then what about when the other person is not there? Eisenberger and colleagues gave heat pain to women who were in long-term relationships, while showing them photographs of their partner, of a stranger and of an object (2011). The relationships had lasted three and a half years on average, from nine months to thirteen years, and three of the women were married. While they were looking at their partner's photograph, the pain was reported as weaker than with the other photographs, and activity in the brain regions involved in pain was also smaller. The region that was strongly active then was one that works when receiving a signal that one is not in danger, and the longer the relationship had lasted, the more strongly it was active. The partner was not in the room. What eased the pain was not the body in front of them but the partner who had come to live, over the years, inside the person who looked.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Because there is someone one can trust "within". I think it's fine even if they are not physically there, even if they are dead. I think even the memory of one's mother from when one was small is fine.

Attachment research has written that as children repeatedly experience their relationship with those who raise them, they come to hold that relationship as expectations about themselves and the other. Bowlby called these expectations internal working models. Internal working model is a term for a relationship remaining in the mind, and it works even when the other is not present. The accumulation includes not only experiences of feeling reassured but also experiences of not being responded to. Seeing trust as expectations accumulated through repetition like this is one aspect seen from the side of attachment research.

The experiment at the head of the chapter was a small study of only seventeen women, and the partners were limited to people the women had rated highly as supportive. The authors think that long-term partners, having accumulated over the years evidence that they stay and are there beside one, probably become stronger safety signals. That a partner's photograph eases pain had also appeared in the authors' earlier study (Master and colleagues, 2009), and this study was also a re-confirmation of it.

Sources

Sources: the photograph experiment at the head of the chapter = Eisenberger, Master, Inagaki, Taylor, Shirinyan, Lieberman & Naliboff (2011) "Attachment figures activate a safety signal-related neural region and reduce pain experience", PNAS 108(28):11721–11726 (full text checked; read in the PDF on the site of a UCLA institute; women in long-term relationships, photographs of the partner, a stranger and an object, and treating the ventromedial prefrontal cortex [VMPFC] as a region involved in safety signals are in the abstract p.11721; lower pain reports with the partner's photograph, smaller activity in pain-related regions [dorsal anterior cingulate cortex, anterior insula], and stronger VMPFC activity with longer relationships [r = 0.43] p.11723; the authors' thought that long-term partners, having accumulated over the years evidence of commitment and of being there, probably become stronger safety signals p.11724; twenty-one completed the procedure, seventeen after excluding four who broke up before it ended, relationship length 3.52 years on average [9 months to 13 years], three married, in "Methods" p.11725; selecting people with high support ratings in the note on p.11723; re-confirming the earlier study [Master et al. 2009] p.11723).

Sources: Ainsworth & Bowlby (1991) "An ethological approach to personality development", American Psychologist 46(4):333–341 (full text checked against the Wayback Machine record of a retyped copy (14 May 2005) that was on the Stony Brook University attachment research site; pages are those of the copy; secure base pp.2, 6, the function of attachment behavior p.5, behavior on reunion p.7, internal working models pp.7–8, the two insecure patterns p.8, expectations of relationships p.9).

Viewpoint 4

Where trust is, seen from the viewpoint of the classification of emotions

There are theories that describe emotions by a small number of basic emotions and their mixtures, and theories that line them up by the emotion systems of the brain. In the chapters that follow, we look at where trust and love have been placed in such ways of dividing.

Where is trust among the emotions?

In the three viewpoints so far, trust and reassurance were seen through their differences from similar things such as expectation and attachment. In the fourth viewpoint, we look at where trust and love have been placed in research that has divided and lined up emotions.

About the classification of emotions, pjdhiro speaks like this.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):As a classification of emotions, what is closest to a definition of Love in the narrow, positive sense is the emotion of trust

Among theories that depict emotions as a small number of basic emotions and their mixtures is Plutchik's theory. In a 2001 paper Plutchik arranged eight basic emotions on a circle and wrote that two adjacent ones, when mixed, become another emotion. In that paper, love is a mixture of joy and acceptance. Acceptance is written as the emotion on meeting a member of one's group, and the word "trusting" is listed as a personality word derived from acceptance. On Plutchik's circle, what is close to trust is placed right next to love.

But there is more than one way to divide emotions. In Panksepp's table of the emotional systems of the mammalian brain, love is placed as one of the emotions arising from the CARE system, and there is no system named trust. Looking at a table listing each researcher's basic emotions, several counted love as basic, but only Plutchik counted acceptance, and no one counted trust. Depending on how emotions are divided, the closeness of trust and love looks different.

The "love" of the research read here is a narrower word than what this reader wants to call Love. Even seen from the side of that narrow love, trust is not placed far away. On Plutchik's circle, love is a mixture that has acceptance, which is close to trust, as half of it. Fredrickson, who defined love as an emotion of pleasant connection born between people, writes that as one experiences that emotion repeatedly with the same person, trust is built. If each way of dividing is seen as touching part of the elephant (the blind men and the elephant), trust is not fixed in one place in the classification of emotions; it can be seen as pointed to from several places as something right beside love. The trust that pjdhiro saw as closest to Love in the narrow, positive sense is in such a place in the research's ways of dividing as well.

Switches of fight and flight, trust and aversion

In the Part 3 chapter "People cut out the world with signals of survival and trust", pjdhiro saw the signals as perhaps the survival signals of fight and flight, and mental signals of pleasure and displeasure such as trust and aversion. On the diagram this view came from, pjdhiro wrote this.

pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):Why can a certain species of cannibalistic shrimp breed, with the female shedding its shell and taking the risk to its life? Isn't it because there are switches of fight (anger) and flight (fear), and one more of trust (liking) and aversion? Simply. And human instinct too.

On Plutchik's circle, anger and fear face each other, and acceptance and disgust face each other. The other facing pairs are joy and sadness, and surprise and anticipation, four pairs in all. Plutchik does not treat two of these pairs specially, apart from the others.

Taking out two pairs is pjdhiro's hypothesis. The figure below draws that hypothesis in a form that overlays the signals of consciousness and the words for emotions. The axis through anger and fear is the survival signal, the axis through trust (acceptance) and disgust is the trust signal, and the other two pairs are drawn as dotted lines. As seen in the Part 3 chapter "Signals well up from inside the body", if the signals of the body well up first and what feeling they are is read afterwards, then the words for emotions on the wheel become names applied afterwards to the movements of the two switches.

About badness and weakness, and forgiveness and salvation, in the figure, pjdhiro speaks like this.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Badness and weakness are what one wants to defend. This is from Shinya Nitta. Not unhealthy defense, but as healthy emotional processing, forgiveness and salvation too. Forgiveness is gratitude and apology. This is also Shinya Nitta.

The movement by which the mind protects itself from anxiety and pain (defense) was seen in the chapter "The mind has mechanisms to protect itself". The side of loosening the guard, accepting one's own weakness and badness, and processing emotions is seen in the chapter "How does one train it, how does one practice?".

Sources

Sources: Plutchik (2001) "Integration, differentiation, and derivatives of emotion", Evolution and Cognition 7(2):114–125 (full text checked against the PDF of the issue published by the Konrad Lorenz Institute; the cone model and opposite emotions pp.117–118, the mixture of joy and acceptance is love p.118, the table of mixtures p.118, emotion and personality words p.119, the table of prototypic adaptations p.117, the table of the emotion sequence p.121, the table of derivatives p.123, difficulty of description p.114, mixtures and conflict p.123).

Sources: Panksepp (2001) "The neuro-evolutionary cusp between emotions and cognitions: Implications for understanding consciousness and the emergence of a unified mind science", Evolution and Cognition 7(2):141–163 (a reprint of a paper published in 2000 in Consciousness & Emotion 1:15–54; full text checked against the PDF of the issue published by the Konrad Lorenz Institute; the table of systems and emotions and the meaning of capitals p.156) / Ortony & Turner (1990) "What's basic about basic emotions?", Psychological Review 97(3):315–331 (full text checked against a copy on the first author's university site; the table listing basic emotions p.316, acceptance only in Plutchik p.315, Shaver et al.'s five p.319, doubts about mixtures p.326).

Sources: Fredrickson (2016) "Love: Positivity resonance as a fresh, evidence-based perspective on an age-old topic", in Barrett, Lewis & Haviland-Jones (eds.) Handbook of Emotions (4th ed.), Guilford Press (full text checked against the pre-publication manuscript on the author's lab site; pages are those of the manuscript; what "I love you" points to p.3, feelings, bonds, intimacy and commitment are made by accumulation p.3, the definition of love p.16, repetition builds trust and loyalty p.19).

To read the originals: the papers of Plutchik and Panksepp can both be read in full in the same issue's PDF published by the Konrad Lorenz Institute.

Overlaying the viewpoints

The outlines of trust and reassurance

The aspects seen from the four viewpoints are overlaid to line up the outlines of trust and reassurance, and looked at again through stages and personality.

What outlines do trust and reassurance show?

The chapters so far looked at trust and reassurance from the four viewpoints of self-actualization, psychological defense mechanisms, theories of the mother–child relationship and the classification of emotions, lining them up with expectation, acceptance, faith, idealization, dependence and attachment. Here, we answer what trust is and what reassurance is. The answer is not a single definition but an outline lining up the aspects touched so far, like the blind men describing the elephant.

Aspects of trust touched:

  • The side of its sources: some of the feeling of trust comes not only from the person in front of one, but from patterns one is not conscious of (Freud, Klein, Jung; the chapter "Where do the feelings of trust and love come from?").
  • Difference from expectation: expectation is the movement in which a self trying to be perfect counts on the other as omnipotent; trust, Shinya Nitta writes, wells up when the other supports one's fragility, and is born from relationships of mutual help.
  • The side of acceptance: pjdhiro also calls self-acceptance trust in oneself, and sees it as the attitude of being convinced of oneself including what one cannot do and one's bad parts (the chapter "How do acceptance and trust differ?").
  • Difference from faith: a way of believing in which the fulfilment of a wish comes to the fore (illusion) dispenses with verifying itself, Freud writes.
  • Difference from idealization: idealization is the movement of selecting and enlarging only the good aspects and keeping the opposite side split off. On the axis in the working notes of "What Is Consciousness" (the side that sees only good outcomes and the side that holds good and bad together), which side trust is on is not yet decided.
  • The side of attachment: expectations about the other and oneself accumulated through repetition. Experiences of not being responded to are included too.
  • Within the classification of emotions: where it is placed differs with the way of dividing, but in several ways of dividing it is placed right beside love (Plutchik, Fredrickson).
  • How pjdhiro applies the word: pjdhiro feels something like a vector heading toward stability and integration, and applies "trust" to it. There are things the person feels as trust that others cannot call so.

Aspects of reassurance touched:

  • Being able to be with anxiety: when one has accepted one's weakness, anxiety arises but one can be with it. pjdhiro sees this as one definition of reassurance (self-acceptance). pjdhiro also says, "It also resembles courage."
  • Dependence and being held: relying and being held is the foothold for the feeling of being able to rely, Winnicott writes. Dependence is not written as something discarded before reaching trust.
  • The need for safety: reassurance as a need for a predictable, orderly, reliable world (Maslow).
  • Reassurance as protection: pjdhiro sees reassurance and safety sought in a spirit of self-protection or opportunism as a state like dependence through psychological defense mechanisms.
  • Someone within: pjdhiro sees one's being able to hold a sense of something being off even with no one nearby as because there is someone one can trust within.
  • The history of the word anshin (reassurance) is on the separate page "What is anshin?" (in Japanese; 🚧 under construction).

Trust and reassurance can be written as two, but may not be separable. With either, taking only one aspect overlooks another.

How do stages and personality change how trust and reassurance look?

The psychology of the premises was also preparation for looking again at trust and reassurance from the viewpoints of stages and personality. Does the same word "trust" point to the same thing whoever says it?

When people say "I trust you", they assume it gets across to the other with the same meaning. Even with the same "I trust that person", someone who says it about a boss they cannot go against may be pointing to being protected as long as they obey. Someone who has everything decided for them may be pointing to not having to decide for themselves, and someone who says it about a person who shares the blame for failures with them may be pointing to their own position being protected. Someone in a relationship where the other also knows their weak points may be pointing to being accepted as they are; someone confident in themselves, to feeling that they could recover even if betrayed; someone with strong anxiety, to being able to calm down when the other is nearby. Domination and submission, dependence, self-protection, acceptance, self-confidence, anxiety and reassurance: what lies behind the word differs from person to person.

Here the views that appeared in the chapters so far are rearranged from the viewpoints of stages and personality.

From the side of stages. In Cook-Greuter's paper, what the word trust points to is written differently by stage.

Stage What the word trust points to (Cook-Greuter's description)
Self-protective Feeling that one alone is different from those around, it becomes "always me against everyone else", and low trust and excessive wariness perpetuate themselves
Rule-oriented Following the rules means being accepted and protected, so one comes to trust the world more. Asking friends about oneself does not make the world collapse, so "I can trust them"
Pluralist What one can trust is one's own experience, sensations, thoughts and feelings here and now

In Cook-Greuter's framework of leadership maturity, the rule-oriented stage is included in the Conformist stage. That what is called trust is written differently by stage can be read as in the same direction as pjdhiro's view that the word trust occurs in every kind of case depending on the subject's state.

The side of reassurance connects to the table in the chapter "What looks threatening changes with the stage of development". If what it is threatening to lose differs, what brings reassurance should also look different. Reading from the table, at the Conformist stage being let into the group, at the Expert stage having one's expertise recognized, and at the Achiever stage being able to move forward may look close to reassurance.

Whether similar feelings can be told apart also connects to the stages. In the table of the chapter "Why are feelings similar to trust hard to tell apart?", telling apart fine differences between similar emotions came at later stages. Whether one can feel trust apart from expectation or faith may also differ by stage.

From the side of personality. pjdhiro sees personality as something that can also be called the strength and tendencies of psychological defense (the chapter "The mind has mechanisms to protect itself"). pjdhiro also gave justice, perfection, affection, value, creativity, knowledge, diversity, joy, power and peace as examples of objects for which one selects "good" and fixes it (the chapter "How do idealization and trust differ?"). pjdhiro also offers the view of what each personality tends to take as its object (power, justice, peace and so on). The objects in which one readily places trust or reassurance may differ by personality.

Stages and personality are both viewpoints that touch different aspects of the elephant. The same "I trust you" may point, for one person, to someone keeping the rules, and for another, to their own sensations here and now. From a single viewpoint, this discrepancy cannot be seen. Adding viewpoints lets one notice that the same word points to different things for different people. That trust and reassurance do not fit into a single definition can also be read like the blind men describing the elephant.

Research examining trust and reassurance by stage or personality has not yet been found.

Sources

Sources: Cook-Greuter (2013) "Nine Levels of Increasing Embrace in Ego Development: A Full-Spectrum Theory of Vertical Growth and Meaning Making", prepublication version, revised Dec. 2013 (full text checked; pages are printed pages; low trust at the Self-protective stage p.25, rule-oriented trust p.28, trust at the Pluralist stage p.56).

Part 4 looked at what trust is and what reassurance is through the premises of the mind and differences from similar things, and lined them up as outlines rather than a single definition. It also looked again through the lenses of stages and personality. The four parts so far were preparation for answering the first question.

Part 5 looks at what this reader wants to call Love, and answers the first question, "What drives us to do projects?"

Part 5

What is Love?

What I want to call Love

What is it that this reader wants to call Love?

If love is seen as one of the warm feelings that well up in the chest, nights like the following no longer count as love. A parent raising a newborn child may be woken again and again in the middle of the night by crying, be sleepy and irritated, with no warm feeling anywhere, and still get up and pick the child up. That night no feeling welled up, yet the body kept moving. Looking back later, people call that love too.

As seen in the chapter at the end of Part 1, "What has the word Love pointed to?", lining up what has been called Love makes it very broad. About how to handle this breadth, pjdhiro talks like this.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):If you list what is called Love under "What is Love?", it becomes broad. It would be good to make that the starting point. Its driving source. Is it the field or the vortex? The hint is the emotions of acceptance and trust. Or the possibility that the same word is applied to the various aspects we applied it to under "What is value?". In a field called Love, fluctuations of Love and collision, Love is born there, and becomes a bundle of Love. Something like that.

Perhaps the same single word is applied to various aspects. People give names to vortices of a size easy to perceive (the fold "Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves"). The narrowest way of cutting out Love is as a feeling, such as falling in love or fondness. What pjdhiro wants to call Love includes those feelings, and is what moves them. pjdhiro is not confident enough to state flatly that this is Love, and that is why this reader writes "what I want to call Love".

🚧 Asking about the driving source of each of the things lined up in the chapter at the end of Part 1 is under construction.

What is what I want to call Love not?

While looking at the schools of strategy in management studies one by one by what they see as the driving source, a question arose with the school that sees strategy as born from power and maneuvering (what Mintzberg and colleagues call the power school (in Japanese)). Are people moving projects by a desire for power or domination being moved by what I want to call Love?

In the summer of 1932, the physicist Einstein, in a project of the League of Nations' institute, asked Freud, who had founded psychoanalysis, a question by letter: is there a way to free human beings from the calamity of war? In September of that year, Freud wrote his reply from Vienna. Having divided human drives into two, those that seek to unite and those that seek to destroy, he wrote that hardly ever does only one of them work alone. He also wrote that even the love drive directed at an object must borrow the drive to master it in order to obtain that object. In this letter, Freud writes of the two as separate drives.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Probably something like a desire for power or domination is in the driving source. Whether that is Love is a very important question, and my guess is that it is one aspect of Love. I think sad things like war happen because of something I want to call Love.

What I want to call Love is not limited to the emotions of romance or liking; emotion is its most easily perceived aspect. Nor does it include only what feels good. It may not even be something to which the yardsticks of good and bad, or rational or not, apply (this hypothesis is left as a question in the chapter "What is still not known"). People are inside it, and they themselves are part of it, so it does not seem to be something one can point to from outside, take out and look at.

Sources

The letters of Einstein and Freud: Albert Einstein & Sigmund Freud, Warum Krieg?, Internationales Institut für geistige Zusammenarbeit, Völkerbund, 1933 (full text checked against a copy of the printed edition on the Internet Archive). Checked were the question in Einstein's letter (Caputh, 30. Juli 1932), "Gibt es einen Weg, die Menschen von dem Verhängnis des Krieges zu befreien?", and, in Freud's reply (Wien, im September 1932), the passage saying that the two drives hardly ever work alone and are mixed with each other (legiert), and the passage saying that the love drive directed at an object (Liebestrieb) must add the drive to master (Bemächtigungstrieb) to obtain the object. The English is Claude's translation.

What aspects does what I want to call Love have?

A vortex is one state of a wave, and a wave one state of the field, and people have given names to the shapes of vortices (the fold "Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves"). Then where among field, wave and vortex is what I want to call Love?

In Shakespeare's Much Ado About Nothing, the young nobleman Claudio accuses his bride Hero of unfaithfulness at the wedding and walks away. A friar who was there advises them to announce that Hero has died, and explains why. While people have something, they do not prize it as much as it is worth. Only when it is lacking and lost do they find the good in it that they could not see while they had it.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):The field may be Love, or the fluctuations and waves of the field may be Love. The figure of producing vortices as if in opposition from exactly opposite directions may be close to Love. I think it is precisely because the phenomenon there is easy to perceive that people lumped it together as romance and love

What I want to call Love has the aspect of a center that does not move and looks as if nothing is there, like the center of a gravity well (the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?"). It also has the aspect of the field people are in and its distortion, and the aspect of the figure of fluctuations of the field and opposing things meeting and producing vortices. Because one is inside, the field itself cannot be observed directly. What can be observed are the things moved by the distortion, and the signals that well up in the body. pjdhiro also estimates that the wave motion that makes up matter and love in the broad sense may follow the same laws, and sees the mind, as an apparatus of interpretation, as perceiving that as love and emotion (see also: Supplementary material — structural similarities with physics).

Another aspect is the stability of being connected without confirming, felt beyond metacognition. This aspect is about a sense pjdhiro felt at the end of observing their own inside.

Metacognition is observing one's own feelings and thoughts as objects. pjdhiro sees this as the work of dividing oneself. You observe feelings. You observe thoughts. Dividing like this, in the end only the observing I remains. When you try to observe that I too, the observing I and the observed I can no longer be divided (the fold "Seeing the invisible center by dividing oneself" in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?").

pjdhiro likens what is felt there to a feeling of calm, as if the presence of a mother, connected by an umbilical cord beyond the top of the head, brings stability (pjdhiro's image in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?"). What lies beyond cannot be seen. pjdhiro also adds that what lies beyond the analogy is not known (the chapter "What is what I want to call Love like?").

pjdhiro writes of not being able to see beyond it as stability, not anxiety.

pjdhiro's experience (translated by Claude):Boundlessness cannot be seen beyond. And yet it is not instability but a sense of stability.

When one cannot see ahead, people usually feel anxiety. What anxiety is was seen in the chapter "What is anxiety?" in Part 4.

That is, apart from the reassurance gained by confirming, there is a reassurance of being connected without confirming. This page calls it "the stability of being connected without confirming". It is one person's report of introspection, and is placed not as evidence but as one aspect of what I want to call Love.

The body receives this stability, and gaps from it, as signals. The gaps are felt as a sense of something being off, or as a lack (the chapter "When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack"). pjdhiro sees it as possible that this sense of stability is being used as the measure by which things are evaluated.

More — the aspects of center, field and vortex

A center that does not move and looks as if nothing is there. In the figure of the gravity well, the center never moves once. Even so, everything around revolves along the hollow that center makes. From oneself standing inside, that center cannot be seen. What pjdhiro has been looking for is a driving source like this center (the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?").

The field people are in, and its distortion. pjdhiro puts forward the hypothesis that sees the universe and us as drifting as if drawn by the distortions of the field of Love (pjdhiro's hypothesis in the same chapter).

The figure of the fluctuation of the field and waves, of opposing things meeting and giving rise to a vortex. It is the process of creation in which the field fluctuates, waves rise, different things meet at an edge and bind as a vortex (the fold "Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves"). pjdhiro sees that because this figure is the easiest to perceive, people probably bundled it as romantic love or love (pjdhiro's view at the head of this chapter).

More — when stability wells up though one cannot see ahead

Though one cannot see ahead, why does stability well up rather than anxiety? The sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?" lines up three threads on why it is stability rather than anxiety (chapter 4, in Japanese). What cannot be seen is not because something is missing but because one is riding on it. Stability comes not from knowing but from being connected. Not seeing ahead becomes anxiety when one sees what lies ahead as other; if one is not cut off, the unseen ahead becomes the side one belongs to. These three are an arrangement that used first-person reports as material, not a proof. In this picture, the one connected to is not seen and has not been confirmed. The reassurance gained by confirming and the stability of being connected without confirming look alike from outside and differ inside.

The dividing line changes from person to person, and even within the same person from time to time, and both may be welling up within one person. At the stage of bearing the anxiety of not seeing ahead without filling it, it can be read that the signal of survival is still sounding and the signal of trust is holding it down. pjdhiro sees wondering what it would be if something welled up in a place where the signal is zero as the noticing of kesson (the fold in the chapter "When signals and thoughts part, one feels a lack"). There is not yet an index for measuring from outside which is welling up and telling them apart.

More — experiments measuring connection lowering the response to unseeable threat

Some research holds that under the uncertainty of not seeing ahead, the stress response is not born after something happens, but is working from the start. Only while one feels safe is that response held down (Brosschot, Verkuil and Thayer 2018). Under the condition that, once a cue appeared, an electric shock came one time in five, while people waited not knowing whether it would come, holding a husband's hand weakened the brain's threat-related responses, and the higher the quality of the relationship, the more they weakened (Coan, Schaefer and Davidson 2006). That holding hands weakens the brain's response to unseeable threat was shown in an experiment with 16 married women.

However, in these experiments the person whose hand is held is visible and being touched. Stability when the other cannot be seen is looked at in the next fold (see also: "What Is Consciousness").

Sources: Brosschot, Verkuil & Thayer (2018) "Generalized Unsafety Theory of Stress", International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health 15(3):464 (full text checked against the public version on PMC; abstract, section 1.2, section 3) / Coan, Schaefer & Davidson (2006) "Lending a hand", Psychological Science 17(12):1032 (full text checked against the PDF of the journal pages on the site of the University of Wisconsin's Center for Healthy Minds, of co-author Davidson; abstract p.1032, threat cues p.1033).

More — can the stability of being connected without confirming be measured?

In the hand-holding experiment, the person whose hand was held was visible and being touched. Then has the stability of being connected to someone unseen been measured? In part, it has.

Attachment research has seen people as holding, in their minds, images of others they can rely on. When they feel threatened, people reach, below the level of consciousness, for the image of someone not in front of them. Even just calling that image to mind brings reassurance, at least for a time. That even with someone unseen, an image in the mind brings reassurance has been shown by experiments up to this point.

However, what has been measured is the effect of images of others who can be named. There are also experiments that measured something close to an other given no name. When people were asked to record, moment by moment, how strongly they felt an invisible presence while watching footage of magnificent nature, the sense lasted from beginning to end, and afterward it was felt not as an agent like a god or Buddha, but as something like the air enveloping nature.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I express this by applying the word Fūjin, the wind god.

The other is an experiment that exposed people, without their noticing, to words suggesting the sacred. In a task of rearranging sentences, words such as spirit, divine and sacred were included in a form that would not be noticed. Then the brain-wave response was measured when people made errors on a subsequent task. This response is held to be linked to bracing against one's own errors. In people who believe in God, this response became smaller. In people who do not, it became larger instead. The authors conclude that for believers, religion may soften anxious responses to one's own errors.

In both experiments the other was not visible and was given no name. However, the nature-footage experiment produced the sense by showing a grand landscape, and did not measure stability itself. The effect in the sacred-words experiment occurs only within a faith the person already holds. pjdhiro's image of the top of the head being connected to something by something like an umbilical cord (the fold in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?") is a stability of connection with an other who cannot be named, welling up where there is neither landscape nor teaching. This lies outside the two experiments.

Mikulincer, Gillath and Shaver (2002) showed that presenting words representing threat too fast to reach consciousness makes mental images (representations) of attachment figures more accessible. This effect occurred only for attachment figures, and replicated across changes of task and experiment. Shaver and Mikulincer (2008) summarize that having people call to mind supportive attachment figures improves mood, compassion and tolerance toward outgroups, and that this also occurs in insecurely attached people. That is, even just evoking, below consciousness, the image of an attachment figure being there may bring reassurance, at least for a time.

Sources: Mikulincer, Gillath & Shaver (2002) "Activation of the attachment system in adulthood: Threat-related primes increase the accessibility of mental representations of attachment figures", Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 83(4):881 / Shaver & Mikulincer (2008) "Augmenting the sense of security in romantic, leader–follower, therapeutic, and group relationships: A relational model of psychological change", in J. P. Forgas & J. Fitness (Eds.), Social Relationships: Cognitive, Affective, and Motivational Processes, Psychology Press, chapter 4 pp.55– (DOI 10.4324/9780203888124-10; pages are the printed pages of the authors' PDF) (both checked against the full text in PDFs on the authors' site: 2002, 2008).

More — experiments that measured something close to an other given no name

Takano and Nomura (2023) showed 77 university students two minutes each of footage of magnificent nature, funny animals and walking in a park, had them tilt a joystick when they felt an "invisible presence" while watching, and at the same time measured skin conductance and pupil size. The invisible presence was explained beforehand as larger than oneself, vast and supernatural, and distinct from the wind or smells one would feel if one went there. With the magnificent footage, this sense rose about 10 seconds after the start and was maintained to the end. In ratings after watching, the presence was felt not as "a superhuman agent like a god or Buddha" but as "not a substance, something like the air enveloping nature". Skin conductance responses occurred often and suddenly with the magnificent footage, while the time to return to baseline was shortest; the authors read this as possibly an expression of a calm mind. However, the authors themselves write that they did not measure stability (homeostasis) itself. That the experimenters gave the frame of the sense in words is also a difference from the image of the umbilical cord that wells up without a name.

pjdhiro expresses this sense, and its being felt as "not a substance, something like the air enveloping nature", with the word Fūjin (main text). What pjdhiro has pointed to with the word Fūjin appears in other writings of pjdhiro's. When overlaying salvation and forgiveness on the Wind God and Thunder God screens, pjdhiro wrote of the Fūjin side: "a vessel of acceptance that enfolds like the wind, the healing of forgiveness. With an ambiguity like superposition. Like a square root." When writing about the difference between intelligence and wisdom, as what is orthogonal to the Thunder God's "power to divide", pjdhiro wrote: "It encompasses survival and money, the mind and trust. Roundness. Fūjin's. Ambiguous. Cannot be divided, makes things undividable", and also: "the power I most want to gain through meditation. Fūjin's." When writing about the subtle signals of the body, pjdhiro wrote: "Of acceptance. The base of trust. Wabi-sabi. A power that enfolds like the wind. Of inclusion." (all translated by Claude)

Inzlicht and Tullett (2010) included words such as spirit, divine and sacred, in a form that would not be noticed, in a sentence-rearranging task, and measured a brain-wave component (the error-related negativity) when people made errors on a subsequent task. In people who believe in God this response became smaller, and in people who do not, it became larger instead. It is an experiment in which the brain's response changed even though the other was not shown, not named, and not noticed by the person. However, it worked only within a system of faith the person already holds.

Zilcha-Mano, Mikulincer and Shaver (2012) had dog or cat owners do a difficult task and compared blood pressure under three conditions: the pet in the room, the pet not in the room but called to mind, and not called to mind. Even when the pet was not in the room, just calling it to mind held down the rise in blood pressure during the task about as much as when it was present. This shows that the body's response changes even with an unseen other, but the other is one's own pet, a nameable other confirmed through daily responsiveness. The review by Mikulincer and Shaver (2020) writes that symbolic figures such as God, the Buddha or the Virgin Mary can also be a safe haven and a secure base. However, it also writes that for insecure people such a figure is merely a reflection of the person's own relational patterns, not an other who overturns those patterns through responsiveness.

In meditation research, there is a study that induced in experts a state in which the boundary between self and world thins, and set brain activity against first-person reports (Dor-Ziderman et al. 2013). The reports included "there was experience, but it had no addressee, it was not tied to a center or a subject" and "resting in experience and being; easy and pleasant". However, what was measured is the direction in which the boundary disappears, not the side of the image of connecting to an unseen beyond while the distinction remains.

Sources: Takano & Nomura (2023) "A closer look at the time course of bodily responses to awe experiences", Scientific Reports 13:22506 (open-access full text checked; methods and rating items p.3, results pp.4–5, discussion p.7) / Inzlicht & Tullett (2010) "Reflecting on God: Religious primes can reduce neurophysiological response to errors", Psychological Science 21(8):1184 (full text checked against the PDF on the author's site; what can be opened now is the Wayback Machine record of the PDF that was on that site (7 November 2014); abstract p.1184, experiment 2 pp.1186–1187) / Zilcha-Mano, Mikulincer & Shaver (2012) "Pets as safe havens and secure bases: The moderating role of pet attachment orientations", Journal of Research in Personality 46(5):571 (full text checked against the PDF on the site of the authors' university lab; conditions p.573, results p.577) / Mikulincer & Shaver (2020) "Enhancing the 'Broaden and Build' cycle of attachment security in adulthood: From the laboratory to relational contexts and societal systems", International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health 17(6):2054 (full text checked against the text on PMC; section 11) / Dor-Ziderman, Berkovich-Ohana, Glicksohn & Goldstein (2013) "Mindfulness-induced selflessness: A MEG neurophenomenological study", Frontiers in Human Neuroscience 7:582 (open-access full text checked; instructions p.4, participants' descriptions p.6).

Sources (pjdhiro's words on Fūjin, in Japanese): salvation and forgiveness = pjdhiro, "Dialogue: 'The trees of the forest', the Wood Wide Web and a yin-yang fractal of 青山常運歩" / intelligence and wisdom = the original text of the copy of pjdhiro's post "The roundness and rotation of the normal distribution" / the subtle signals of the body = the original text of the copy of pjdhiro's post "Aufheben — separation and inclusive integration".

More — a measure that tells of a gap

By what logic the signal of trust wells up is not yet known. About what people compare things against when they feel something is off, or good or bad, pjdhiro estimates like this.

pjdhiro's estimate (translated by Claude):Maybe part of it is evaluating by similarity to the experience of reassurance. From when we were fetuses. When I do metacognition I feel that kind of stability. When only the one who observes remains, I feel something like a connection with others somewhere. Like a wormhole. Maybe the gap from that sense is used as the measure of a sense of something being off, or of good and bad. Beauty, goodness and so on.

A wormhole is something considered in physics as a bridge connecting two distant places (chapter 4 of the sister reader "Isn't Love like gravity?", in Japanese).

Sources

Much Ado About Nothing: William Shakespeare, Much Ado About Nothing, the lines of Friar Francis in Act 4, Scene 1 ("for it so falls out / That what we have we prize not to the worth / Whiles we enjoy it, but being lack'd and lost, / Why, then we rack the value, then we find / The virtue that possession would not show us / Whiles it was ours."). Checked against the text in MIT's The Complete Works of William Shakespeare. The retelling in the main text is Claude's.

What is what I want to call Love like?

How can something one is inside and cannot see be understood through an analogy?

pjdhiro has likened the stability felt beyond metacognition to the umbilical cord and the mother (the stability of being connected without confirming). About that analogy, pjdhiro adds this.

pjdhiro's image (translated by Claude):As an expression I used the analogy of the umbilical cord and the mother, but a fetus probably does not recognize that there is a mother in the first place. In the same way, the field or the great ocean is something one does not know.

pjdhiro has used many analogies: gravity; the sea, waves and vortices; a field made of grains; the umbilical cord; the wormhole. The analogy of gravity shows that an unmoving center moves everything around it, and that from inside, its distortion cannot be seen. The analogy of the sea, waves and vortices shows that it is the vortex that gets a name, and that vortices are continuous with waves, and waves with the sea. Each analogy is one aspect of what it points to, and what the field or the great ocean beyond the analogy is remains unknown. pjdhiro does not settle on any one of them, and wants to call the whole of the aspects lined up here Love. This is similar to the way physics has likened light both to particles and to waves, and has used both without settling on one.

More — the analogies and what they are meant to show
Analogy What it is meant to show Read
Gravity (the gravity well, the gravitational field) An unmoving center moves everything around it. From inside, the distortion cannot be seen Isn't Love like gravity?
The sea, waves and vortices Vortices are continuous with waves, and waves with the sea, and it is the vortex that gets a name. What I want to call Love may be the sea, or the wave, or the figure that produces vortices Words are names for the vortices seen between the waves
A field made of grains A field in which waves appear, as if made of grains of Love pjdhiro's hypothesis
The umbilical cord Connected without confirming. What lies beyond cannot be seen, and yet one is stable The stability of being connected without confirming
The wormhole Something like a connection with others, felt when only the one who observes remains A measure that tells of a gap
More — placing the explorations so far on the single picture of the gravity well

Let us place the explorations so far on the single picture of the gravity well (in Japanese), the well in the figure in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?". Which part of the picture, whether the shape of the hollow a mass makes, its center, or the picture as a whole, corresponds to what I want to call Love is not yet known. The four explorations look at the same picture from different sides. pjdhiro sees oneself, standing in the hollow, as also in the picture, and that self as being among people.

Kesson-Driven Thinking A logic in which 0 and a value coexist The center is read as 0 in cognition, yet the body receives the size of the hollow
