Seen from philosophy

How does this reader's way of seeing look when seen from the viewpoint of philosophy?

The Japanese word for philosophy, tetsugaku, was made to translate the English word philosophy, which goes back to the Greek philosophia, the love of wisdom. Dictionaries explain it as the study that seeks the fundamental principles of the world and of human life.

Hearing that it seeks fundamental principles, one may think of it as a study that leaves action behind and works out correct answers in the head. In Ming China, Xu Ai, a disciple of Wang Yangming, asked him this. People know that they should be devoted to their father and follow their elder brother, yet there are times when they cannot do it. So are knowing and doing not two things? Wang Yangming answered that to know and not to do is simply not yet to know. Knowing something and still not being able to do it is familiar to everyone, and Wang Yangming saw it as not yet knowing.

Philosophy, too, has currents that do not cut knowing off from action, and currents that do not first divide the knowing self from the known thing. This chapter lines up this reader's way of seeing with four currents of philosophy: pragmatism, pure experience, existentialism and the Eastern classics. It lines up, in the words of the original texts, where they look close and where they look different. At the end of the chapter there is a guide for reading further in the original texts.

Checking happens within practice

This reader has cited many theories, from physics, psychology, management studies and elsewhere. What are those references for? pjdhiro says this.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):They are references for explaining my experience, not proof that my experience is right. I think I am taking the stance of presenting a hypothesis that my experience might be useful to you as well, and that its verification is something found in your own practice. Roughly speaking, I am just introducing myself: this is what I am like.

This stance is close to the current of philosophy called pragmatism. In an 1878 paper, Peirce treated belief as something that sets up in us a rule of action, that is, a habit. And he wrote that the meaning of an idea is the whole of the effects it is conceived to have in practice. In his 1907 lectures, James named Peirce's paper as the origin of this idea, and called the method not a particular result but an attitude of orientation: the attitude of looking away from first principles and looking towards results and facts. He also wrote that truth is not a property that belongs to an idea, but a process in which it comes to be verified through events.

In a paper of the year before, 1877, Peirce wrote that the irritation of doubt causes inquiry, and that the object of inquiry is to settle opinion. This reader has valued holding questions that are not yet solved without hurrying to settle them. pjdhiro sees these two as compatible, because after a question of inquiry has arisen, one may hold it for a long time.

Dewey's view, that perplexity guides thinking, was seen in the column "Types of thinking, and design rules". Peirce's creative love and abduction can be read on the separate page "Peirce's creative love, and abduction" (in Japanese).

The closeness to the Eastern classics is looked at in the fourth section of this chapter, "In the Eastern classics".

Sources

C. S. Peirce, "The Fixation of Belief", Popular Science Monthly 12, 1877; "How to Make Our Ideas Clear", same journal 12, 1878. Both were checked against the text as reprinted in Cohen (ed.), Chance, Love, and Logic (1923; public domain; a scan of a University of Toronto copy on the Internet Archive). The irritation of doubt and the object of inquiry ("the sole object of inquiry is the settlement of opinion") are on p.16, belief as setting up a habit ("The essence of belief is the establishment of a habit") on p.41, and the rule that the meaning of an idea is the whole of its conceived effects on p.45.

The explanation of philosophy at the head of the chapter: Digital Daijisen and Seisenban Nihon Kokugo Daijiten, "哲学" (philosophy) (text checked on Kotobank; the word comes from philosophia, "love of wisdom", and means the study that seeks the fundamental principles of the world, human life and so on).

The exchange between Xu Ai and Wang Yangming at the head of the chapter: see the sources of the section "In the Eastern classics" in this chapter (Chuanxi lu, Book 1, section 5).

William James, Pragmatism: A New Name for Some Old Ways of Thinking, Longmans, Green, 1907. Checked against the public-domain text on Project Gutenberg. The place where he names Peirce's 1878 paper and the attitude of orientation ("THE ATTITUDE OF LOOKING AWAY FROM FIRST THINGS, PRINCIPLES, 'CATEGORIES,' SUPPOSED NECESSITIES; AND OF LOOKING TOWARDS LAST THINGS, FRUITS, CONSEQUENCES, FACTS.") are in Lecture II, "What Pragmatism Means"; truth happening to an idea ("Truth HAPPENS to an idea.") is in Lecture VI, "Pragmatism's Conception of Truth".

Subject and object divide afterwards

With the analogies of water and waves, and of waves and vortices, this reader has seen that what we divide and name is not apart. In philosophy, there were people who thought about a similar place with the words "pure experience". While thinking about this chapter, pjdhiro posed this question about two of them.

pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):James's unity of subject and object and Nishida Kitarō's seem to be saying the same thing.

He went on to add where he thinks misunderstanding is likely.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I think there are words like fusion of subject and object, or similar words, but I don't understand them in detail as philosophical terms. I do think misunderstandings are common about these points: that it is non-dual; that what is called pure experience rises up like a vortex between the waves; and that once you include the field behind the vortex, such as the sea and the wind that give rise to the waves, subject and object are hard to separate.

Neither of them sets the seeing self and the seen thing apart from the start. In a 1904 paper, James called the primal stuff of the world "pure experience", and explained knowing as a relation between portions of it. He gave the image of the experience of one room that stands at the crossing of two lines, the history of a thing and the history of a mind, and is counted twice. He also wrote that the experience of the present field is not yet either subject or object. In An Inquiry into the Good (1911), Nishida Kitarō called pure experience the experience of the instant of seeing a colour or hearing a sound, when there is not yet subject or object. He also included experiences that go on with attention held on things, such as climbing a cliff with all one's might, or a musician playing a piece he knows well. In this book, Nishida cites James's paper by name. "Unity of subject and object" (shukaku gōitsu) is Nishida's phrase.

Neither of them writes of pure experience as a state that is mixed together and without distinctions. Nishida writes that experience has distinctions in itself. James, too, writes that it is both one and many, only that the points of distinction and of sameness have not yet been grasped.

There are differences, too. James did not set the world on a single foundation, and took the transitions that connect one experience to another as real in themselves. Nishida saw the universe as the activity of a single reality, and took opposition and unity as two sides of the same thing.

Laid over this page's image, Nishida's single reality looks close to the field, including the sea and the wind behind the vortex. James sets no foundation behind, so at that point he moves away. Also, James's pure experience is on the side of the flow to which no name has yet been fitted, and the shapes that have taken a name and hardened are on the side cut out from it. Whether to fit pure experience to the vortex or to the flow is not settled as one even between the two of them.

At the end of An Inquiry into the Good, Nishida also sees love as the unity of subject and object. To love a thing is to cast off the self and become one with the other; to love a flower is to become one with the flower, he writes. And he continues that to know a thing, one must love it.

The Japanese philosopher Watsuji Tetsurō, too, looked at people from a starting point that does not first divide subject and object. In the manuscript of Fūdo (Climate and Culture), Watsuji writes that the "feeling" of feeling cold is not a single point that enters into a relation towards the cold air, but is itself already a relation. To divide "we" and "the cold air" as existing separately on their own is "a misunderstanding", he says; when we feel the cold, we already dwell under the cold outside and have gone out into the cold (translated by Claude).

In the manuscript of Ethics as the Study of Man (Ningen no gaku to shite no rinrigaku), he starts from the fact that the word ningen (human being) has been used in the double sense of the world of people and of a person. The between of thing and thing is distance; remove the distance and the between disappears too. By contrast, he writes that the between of person and person "can be at once a separation of distance and a unity without separation" (translated by Claude). The between here does not mean space, but intercourse and living relationship.

Watsuji's climate looks at people including the field, such as the weather and the land. This looks close to what was in pjdhiro's view: once you include the field behind the vortex, such as the sea and the wind, subject and object are hard to separate. Seeing the between of person and person as something that can be one while being apart also looks close to this page's view that what we divide and name is not apart.

In detail: where do James and Nishida part?

The differences between the two are lined up in the words of the original texts. The arrangement is Claude's.

What is looked at James Nishida
Subject and object One and the same experience is counted twice, in two contexts. The experience of the present field has not yet become either subject or object The distinction of subject and object is a relative form that arises when the unity of experience is lost
The whole Sets no foundation such as a substance or the Absolute. The unity of the world has not yet been fully experienced The universe is the single activity of a single reality
Connection The relations that connect experiences are themselves experienced realities Opposition and unity are the same thing seen from both sides
Purity A relative term: the proportion of sensation not yet put into words that remains Decided by the strictness of unity; unity and disunity differ in degree
Where it goes To the theory of knowing and truth, and to pragmatism To the good (the realization of personality) and to religion

In research, many have distinguished the two by saying that James's pure experience is material before it is confined by concepts, while Nishida's pure experience is something unifying that contains concepts within it. Arai Jun (2024) holds that this does not follow James's intention, and places the root of the difference in the contrast between James, who sees the parts of experience as always in transition, and Nishida, who sees opposition and the unity behind it.

Sources

The primal stuff and knowing, the room counted twice, and the present field that is not yet either subject or object ("only virtually or potentially either object or subject as yet") are in §I and §III of "Does 'Consciousness' Exist?"; the definition of radical empiricism, in which relations too are experienced, and the image of the mosaic with no foundation, are in §I and §VII of "A World of Pure Experience"; the flow that is both one and many, and the relativity of purity, are at the opening of "The Thing and Its Relations".

Existentialism

On existentialism too, pjdhiro sees that this theory has that viewpoint.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):This theory does have an existentialist viewpoint. Placing it from that viewpoint might be easy to understand for people who like philosophy.

On the other hand, he also sees the question at the opening of this reader and the question of existentialism as out of line with each other.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):There was a period when I explored existential questions, but I also think they are out of line with this question.

Existentialism is not the name of one school. The entry on existentialism in the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (Aho 2023) lists six ideas that overlap in this current, which includes both religious people and atheists: meaninglessness (nihilism); starting not from detached observation but from within the situation one is placed in; existence preceding essence; freedom; authenticity; and ethics.

The entry introduces the idea that existence precedes essence as one that began with Heidegger and was restated by Sartre. It is the idea that a person is not determined by an essence given in advance, and keeps making themselves through choices within situations. According to this entry, what existentialism asks about is the freedom and responsibility of a person who makes themselves in this way, meaninglessness, anxiety, death and authenticity.

What the two questions at the opening, "What drives me to do projects?" and "Why do I try to become something different from what I was?", ask about is what drives that movement, and why. Where they overlap is that the "try to become" of the second question overlaps with the idea of continuing to make oneself. This reader has seen the driving source as something a person cannot consciously place first, but which may have been placed first unconsciously (What does development point to?). In wording, this points in the opposite direction from the idea of making oneself through choices. However, the same entry also arranges existentialism as something that does not deny facticity, such as the body and desires and the social and historical context, but questions things anew while bound by it. pjdhiro's view, that what had been placed first becomes, in the eyes of others, the person's own fault, can also be read as looking at the same scene from the other side as the existentialist view that assigns responsibility to choice.

What looks close is the view of anxiety. In The Concept of Anxiety (1844), Kierkegaard distinguished anxiety from fear. Fear and things like it are directed at something definite. Anxiety, he writes, is freedom's actuality as the possibility of possibility. He also says that anxiety is a sympathetic antipathy and an antipathetic sympathy. At the end of the same book, he makes learning to be anxious an adventure that everyone must go through, so as not to perish either by never having been anxious or by sinking into anxiety. And he writes that whoever has learned to be anxious in the right way has learned the highest thing.

This reader has seen anxiety not as something to erase or calm, but as an important emotion (What is anxiety?). Kierkegaard's view of anxiety as something to be learned looks close to that.

Sources

Kevin Aho, "Existentialism", Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, 2023. Checked against the entry in the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy. The six overlapping ideas are at the opening; the idea that existence precedes essence, its restatement, and facticity are in §3; the freedom and responsibility, meaninglessness, anxiety and authenticity that the questions are directed at are at the opening and in §4 and §5.

Søren Kierkegaard, Begrebet Angest (The Concept of Anxiety), 1844. Checked against the text of volume 4 of Søren Kierkegaards Skrifter published by the Royal Danish Library (the Danish original). The translations are Claude's. The distinction of anxiety from fear, freedom's actuality as the possibility of possibility ("Angest er Frihedens Virkelighed som Mulighed for Muligheden"), and sympathetic antipathy are in Chapter 1, §5; learning to be anxious, and that whoever has learned to be anxious in the right way has learned the highest thing ("hvo der derfor lærte at ængstes retteligen, han har lært det Høieste"), are in Chapter 5.

In the Eastern classics

Taking pragmatism as knowing within action, pjdhiro has also turned his eyes to the Eastern classics.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):If pragmatism means knowing within action, then the Yangming school's unity of knowledge and action is close too. I like the Yangming school better. I'm not good with ritual things or formal things. I like the Doctrine of the Mean and the Great Learning. Laozi and Zhuangzi too.

Here, the three that were named, the Yangming school's unity of knowledge and action, the Doctrine of the Mean and the Great Learning, and Laozi and Zhuangzi, are looked at in the words of the original texts. When the three classics are lined up, three things come up again and again: that the outline of words is not fixed, that what has been divided is not apart, and that knowing is not apart from action and long training.

The words of Wang Yangming of the Ming dynasty remain in the Chuanxi lu (Instructions for Practical Living), recorded by his disciples. The unity of knowledge and action appears in the exchange with his disciple Xu Ai seen at the head of the chapter. After answering that to know and not to do is simply not yet to know, Wang Yangming cites the phrase from the Great Learning, as when we hate a bad smell, and as when we love a beautiful colour, and says that seeing the beautiful colour belongs to knowing and loving it belongs to doing, but that when one sees it, one already loves it. He also says that to know pain, one must oneself have been in pain. And he restates it: knowing is the beginning of doing, and doing is the completion of knowing.

Wang Yangming does not forbid speaking of knowing and doing separately. He sees it as a way of speaking that made up for a one-sidedness: to people who act without thinking, one speaks of knowing, and to people who only think and do not act, one speaks of doing. People today wait to know first and then act, so they never act all their lives and never know all their lives. The unity of knowledge and action is a medicine for that illness, he says. He also says that only by polishing oneself where one meets affairs does one become settled both in stillness and in movement.

In not cutting knowing off from action, Wang Yangming looks close to Peirce and James. What looks different is the direction. Pragmatism was an inquiry that checks things in the experience to come. Wang Yangming's unity of knowledge and action speaks of returning to "the original substance of knowing and doing", before it is separated by selfish desires. On form, too, Wang Yangming rejects trying to look good from outside, and says that people who have come along only with outer ornament fall down when they grow old, like a tree without roots transplanted to the waterside. But he does not say that one need not learn the forms of conduct. He takes the mind of true filial devotion as the root and the detailed forms as branches and leaves, and says that first there is the root, and then the branches and leaves. What he rejects is form without roots. A reading that takes form itself as unnecessary is out of line with the original text.

The Doctrine of the Mean and the Great Learning were both originally chapters of the Book of Rites. Zhu Xi (1130–1200) annotated them together with the Analects and the Mencius, and made them the Four Books.

Chapter 1 of the Doctrine of the Mean calls the state before pleasure, anger, sorrow and joy have arisen the Mean (zhong), and calls it harmony (he) when they have arisen and all hit their due measure. In his commentary, Zhu Xi writes that although the Mean and harmony differ as stillness and movement, they are in fact not two things. Chapter 12 says that the way of the superior man begins between ordinary husband and wife, and that at its utmost there is something even the sage does not know. Chapter 20 sets five things in one sequence: learn widely, inquire closely, reflect carefully, discern clearly, practise earnestly. It also says that some know from birth, some know by learning, and some know through hardship, but once they come to know, it is the same.

The passage on making the intention sincere in the Great Learning says that making the intention sincere is not deceiving oneself, as when we hate a bad smell, and as when we love a beautiful colour. This is the phrase that Wang Yangming cited for the unity of knowledge and action. The inscription said to have been carved on King Tang's bathing basin, "If you can renew yourself one day, renew yourself day by day, and again renew yourself each day", is also cited in the Great Learning (translated by Claude).

The commentary that the Mean and harmony are not two things looks like a way of speaking of the same shape as this page's view that what we divide and name is not apart. The view of looking at the state where pleasure, anger, sorrow and joy have not yet arisen could be lined up with pjdhiro's image of shikan, setting the mind to zero to make it easier to tell changes apart (Isn't Love like gravity?). Saying that at its utmost there is something even the sage does not know looks close to the stance of not thinking one has understood.

There are also places that look different. The Great Learning sets out an order by going back step by step: those who wished to bring order to the world first ordered their states; those who wished to order their states first regulated their families; and so on. Zhu Xi read these eight items as, to some degree, separate stages in time. Wang Yangming read them as having an order, yet as a single whole of effort that cannot be divided into before and after. Since Love-Driven Development does not take the form of a procedure (What does development point to?), Wang Yangming's reading looks closer.

The Laozi and the Zhuangzi are together called Lao-Zhuang. Both are books that are hard to see as the work of one person. For the Laozi, it is thought that a collection of sayings attributed to Laozi began to be written down in the late fifth century BCE and was almost settled by the middle of the third century BCE. The present Zhuangzi is the book that Guo Xiang of the Western Jin (died 312) arranged into thirty-three chapters, and the seven Inner Chapters are the ones that have been held to go back to Zhuangzi's time.

Chapter 1 of the Laozi begins: the way that can be told is not the constant way; the name that can be named is not the constant name. Chapter 25 writes: I do not know its name, so I style it "the way", and forcing a name on it, I call it "great". Chapter 2 says that being and non-being give rise to each other, and difficult and easy complete each other.

"The Adjustment of Controversies" (Qiwulun) in the Zhuangzi says that "that" comes out of "this", and "this" too depends on "that". In the dream of the butterfly in the same chapter, Zhuang Zhou, who became a butterfly in a dream, wakes and says he does not know whether Zhou dreamed he was a butterfly or the butterfly dreamed it was Zhou; then the passage concludes that between Zhou and the butterfly there must surely be a distinction. In "The Nourishment of the Lord of Life" (Yangshengzhu), the cook Ding says that at first he saw nothing but the ox, after three years he no longer saw the whole ox, and now he meets it with his spirit and not with his eyes. Even so, each time he comes to a difficult place where the sinews gather, he becomes cautious, fixes his eyes on it and slows his hand. In "External Things" (Waiwu), among the Miscellaneous Chapters, there is a passage: the fish trap is for getting the fish, and once you have the fish you forget the trap; words are for getting the meaning, and once you have the meaning you forget the words.

The phrase "forcing a name on it" looks close to this page's view that people fit names to vortices of a size they can easily recognize. The fish trap and the fish is an image pointing the same way as the finger pointing at the moon. The conclusion that between Zhou and the butterfly there must surely be a distinction looks like a way of writing that does not let not-two be read as melting into one. The story of the cook Ding can be read not as a story of procedure but as one of a stance acquired through long repetition.

Chapter 48 of the Laozi says that those who learn increase day by day, and those who practise the way decrease day by day, and that decreasing and decreasing again, one reaches non-action (wuwei). It goes on: through non-action, nothing is left undone. It is a way of speaking that does not mean doing nothing, but that where one does not act, there is nothing that is not done. pjdhiro sees this as letting go of contrivance and control and riding on opportunities like fluctuations of the field, and says that Peirce and he himself are saying the same thing.

That the outline of words is not fixed, that what has been divided is not apart, and that knowing is not apart from action and long training: the next chapter looks at these three, which come up again and again in the three classics, through the viewpoint of Zen.

Seen from Zen

How does this reader's way of seeing look when seen from the viewpoint of Zen?

Zen gives the impression of a practice of sitting quietly and emptying the mind. The Ten Oxherding Pictures by Kuoan, a Zen monk of the Song dynasty, depicts practice as ten scenes of searching for an ox and bringing it home. The preface to the first scene, setting out to search for the ox, begins: it has never been lost, so why search for it? The person who set out to search brings the ox home, and before long neither the ox nor the self can be seen. The path does not end there. In the ninth scene, one returns to the origin, and things are seen as they are: the water is of itself boundless, the flowers are of themselves red. In the tenth scene, one enters the town, lined with wine shops and fish stalls, carrying a gourd. It is a path that does not end at the point of emptying the mind, but goes on to returning to oneself as one is, and showing that self in the reality of life in the town. The Ten Oxherding Pictures teaches not attaining an ideal self somewhere far away, but realizing oneself as one is.

Here and there, this reader has cited words of Zen and Buddhism. pjdhiro sees the teachings of Zen as a reference for this reader. And he sees Zen as a way, with a history, of developing one's own spirit. So it is looked at through the viewpoint of Zen too. First, the places of the words cited so far are lined up.

  • The finger pointing at the moon: the Buddhist parable that the finger pointing at the moon is not the moon itself (the finger pointing at the moon).
  • Water and waves are not apart: even when water rises into waves in the wind, the waves are not something apart from the water. It is the analogy that even if we divide them in two and give them names, they are not apart. It is an analogy not from a book of the Zen school but from a treatise of Mahāyāna Buddhism (water and waves are not apart).
  • Shikan: the work of watching the inside of oneself as if pointing at it with the mind. Shikan is a word for practice found widely in Buddhism, and was systematized in the Tiantai school. It does not belong to the Zen school alone (Isn't Love like gravity?, and the chapter of this part "How does one train it, how does one practice?").
  • The Heart Sutra: a sutra that negates the pairs one by one, "不生不滅 不垢不浄 不増不減" (neither arising nor ceasing, neither defiled nor pure, neither increasing nor decreasing). It is not a sutra of the Zen school alone, but one that has been recited in many schools, not only the Zen school (From emotion processing to what is not two in the background and history).

Some things outside the Zen school are also lined up here, because pjdhiro sees them as connected with Zen.

Of these, pjdhiro sees the Heart Sutra as follows.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I also think the Heart Sutra is the closest to this theory. If someone asked, "Is this a commentary on the Heart Sutra?", I couldn't deny its influence.

🚧 Under construction: organizing the teachings of Zen as knowledge and lining them up with this reader's way of seeing will be written after checking the original sources.

Sources

Text checked against the digital edition on CBETA. The names of the ten scenes (尋牛 searching for the ox, 見跡 seeing the traces, 見牛 seeing the ox, 得牛 catching the ox, 牧牛 herding the ox, 騎牛歸家 riding the ox home, 忘牛存人 the ox forgotten, the self remains, 人牛俱忘 both self and ox forgotten, 返本還源 returning to the origin, 入鄽垂手 entering the market with helping hands) are the titles of the sections; the preface to the first scene, "從來不失，何用追尋？" (it has never been lost, so why search for it?), is at X64n1269_p0773c02; the preface to the ninth scene, "本來清淨，不受一塵" (originally pure, it receives not a speck of dust), and the verse "水自茫茫花自紅" (the water is of itself boundless, the flowers are of themselves red) are at p0774c20–c23; the preface to the tenth scene, "提瓢入市，䇿杖還家。酒肆魚行，化令成佛" (carrying a gourd, he enters the market; leaning on his staff, he returns home; among wine shops and fish stalls, he transforms them into buddhas), is at p0775a08. The translations are Claude's.

What is this view good for, and in what situations is it useful?

pjdhiro says that readers who have heard this far will probably ask the following.

pjdhiro's question (translated by Claude):So what's it good for? How do you use it?

In work and in society, many ways of doing things come up in conversation: reproducibility, best practices, formulas for success, business plans. Hearing such words, one comes to think that a useful way of thinking is the shape of a way of doing things that worked, and that if one copies that shape, it will work the same way elsewhere. The physicist Feynman, in a talk given at the 1974 commencement of the California Institute of Technology, told of people on islands in the South Seas, called the cargo cult. As Feynman tells it, during the war airplanes landed on the islands and brought a great deal of goods. Wanting the same thing to happen again, the people built something like a runway, lit fires along its sides, made a man sit in a wooden hut, fitted him with wooden headphones and bamboo antennas, and waited for the airplanes. The form was perfect, yet no airplanes landed, Feynman says. Feynman made this an image of science that follows the rules and forms of research but is missing something essential. In ways of working too, the same thing can happen if, before copying a shape that worked, one does not check in what situation that shape worked.

The use of this view is to check what one has received before applying theories or instructions. Part 6 applied this to checking words one has received ("Where are the words of business misunderstood?") and to giving something form ("Where does design come in?"), and looked at what changes when one checks in "What changes when the input is arranged?". This chapter looks at it from the side of in what situations, and under what conditions, it is useful.

The ideas of management studies and project management each have situations where they work well. The table in the Part 1 chapter "What has management studies seen as the driving source?" lined up representative ideas together with the situations where they are effective. Lining up in what situations and under what conditions this view is useful makes the difference from them easier to see. pjdhiro sees the situations where it is useful as follows.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I think it is a viewpoint useful to those who commission projects. Also, for example, when one wants to process the emotion of anxiety in a mature form that is not defense (impatience or evading responsibility).

pjdhiro also sees situations where it is useful for readers outside Japan.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):For people in cultures other than Japan who are interested in Zen, I think it would be useful as a different viewpoint. People interested in meditation or mindfulness, for example.

The first is the situation of the side that commissions a project. The second is the situation in which, when anxiety wells up, one wants to process the emotion without relying on the protections of impatience or evading responsibility. The view that anxiety is not something to erase was seen in the chapter "What is anxiety?", and the mechanisms of protection in the chapter "The mind has mechanisms to protect itself". The third that pjdhiro named is the situation in which people in cultures other than Japan who are interested in Zen, meditation or mindfulness come across this view as a different viewpoint. The places where this reader cited words of Zen and Buddhism are lined up in this part's chapter "Seen from Zen".

The fourth is the situation of making decisions.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):It affects the quality of decision-making. Because one does not get caught by one side of things. It is like personal security that prevents exploitative intrusions such as fraud, exploitation, glossing over and drawing others in. Put another way, even just separating things and making them clear, that understanding can be used as a tool. But the decision-maker has to judge whether to use it or not.

pjdhiro added the following about what the second half of the card meant to convey.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):If one only uses a concept as a tool, "breaking it down and making it clear" for understanding may be enough. But whether that concept is fit as a tool for a solution can be grasped three-dimensionally, from a multi-axis viewpoint, by being able to handle what was separated abstractly, inclusively and non-dually, so as to embrace it.

That is: if one cuts things apart and makes them clear, they can be used as tools. But to judge whether a tool fits the solution, handling what was cut apart so as to wrap it up lets one see it three-dimensionally, from several axes.

Then what about what one takes as value when deciding? pjdhiro sees the character of their own decision-making as follows.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):As my character, I keep an eye on both the for-profit and the non-profit. I have finance and accounting as a foundation. Also, I make project decisions as if pushing multi-axis values, like a radar chart, in the z direction. My sense of values about value may be a little multi-axis.

Finance and accounting are tools that measure value with the yardstick of money. What deciding by the yardstick of money alone can break was seen in Part 6, in "Profit and the long-term question". In the public post "A 60/40 portfolio for life" (in Japanese), pjdhiro also writes that "for-profit and non-profit are difficult", and sees hints of the right answer in an uncorrelated direction, like an imaginary axis.

🚧 Under construction: cases and images that support this fourth situation will be added. What this view brings in each situation, and how it differs from the effective situations lined up in the Part 1 table, will also be written.

Apart from situations, one can also look from the side of the skills that will be needed from now on. The World Economic Forum, in its report The Future of Jobs Report 2025 (2025), showed the skills that will be core in 2030, from a survey of employers around the world: skills already considered core today and expected to be used even more by 2030. The ten the report's text names are AI and big data, analytical thinking, creative thinking, resilience, flexibility and agility, technological literacy, leadership and social influence, curiosity and lifelong learning, systems thinking, talent management, and motivation and self-awareness.

Looking at the report's figure "Core skills in 2030", pjdhiro wrote the following.

pjdhiro's thoughts (translated by Claude):I would be glad if this helps in acquiring these new skills.

Of the ten, the six whose topics overlap with chapters of this reader are the following. Whether reading it makes one acquire the skill has not been checked.

  • Motivation and self-awareness: emotional metacognition, which watches one's own feelings as an object ("Why are feelings similar to trust hard to tell apart?"), and watching the inside of oneself through shikan (the next chapter, "How does one train it, how does one practice?").
  • Resilience, flexibility and agility: the passage that sees resilience not only as a person's own strength but as a flow of recovery that happens after adversity together with support from those around ("Resilience"), and this chapter's second situation (processing anxiety without relying on protections).
  • Curiosity and lifelong learning: pjdhiro's view of continuing to debug oneself ("The answer of the second half").
  • Analytical thinking: cutting things apart and making them clear (this chapter's fourth situation).
  • Systems thinking: seeing from several axes, so as to wrap up what was cut apart (this chapter's fourth situation).
  • Creative thinking: the shape things take when they come into being ("What shape does creation take?"). The general definitions and research on creative thinking, and its relation to the thinking that forms hypotheses, are lined up on a separate page, "What is creative thinking?" (in Japanese).
More — how the report divided and chose the skills

In its survey of employers (Future of Jobs Survey 2024), the report asked two things about 26 skills. One is whether the employer considers the skill core in 2025. The other is whether the employer expects the use of the skill to increase by 2030. Figure 3.6, "Core skills in 2030", takes the share for the former on the horizontal axis and the share for the latter on the vertical axis, and calls the upper-right section, where both are above the median, "core skills in 2030". The ten above are the ones the text names as that section. Their English names are: AI and big data, analytical thinking, creative thinking, resilience, flexibility and agility, technological literacy, leadership and social influence, curiosity and lifelong learning, systems thinking, talent management, motivation and self-awareness.

The skills are chosen from the World Economic Forum's Global Skills Taxonomy and are divided into clusters in Table A2 of the appendix. Seen by cluster, the ten are: three cognitive skills (analytical thinking, creative thinking, systems thinking); three in self-efficacy (resilience, flexibility and agility; curiosity and lifelong learning; motivation and self-awareness); two technology skills (AI and big data, technological literacy); one in working with others (leadership and social influence); and one management skill (talent management). The six read as overlapping with this reader fall in the clusters of cognitive skills and self-efficacy.

Empathy and active listening is considered core in 2025 by 50% of employers, and in the text it falls in the section of skills that are core now but are not expected to grow much by 2030 (lower right).

Sources: World Economic Forum (2025) The Future of Jobs Report 2025 (full text checked against the report PDF; shares of core skills in 2025, Figure 3.3, p.35; text on core skills in 2030, p.40; Figure 3.6, p.41; skill clusters, Table A2, p.101).

Of the rest, pjdhiro sees that this view can also be used for leadership and social influence in the cluster of working with others, and for talent management in the management cluster.

pjdhiro also sees, though, that it is not needed in every kind of work. There are businesses that make a profit even when one moves without thinking, on bare desire for control, and there are areas that go well without knowing this view. This view comes alive when the situation that one's way of doing things presupposes and the situation in front of one are out of line. As in the table of the Part 1 chapter "What has management studies seen as the driving source?", every idea has situations where it works. pjdhiro sees that in work that delivers fixed things to fixed customers, the current way tends to work as it is, while in work where the other side is broad and what they want is hard to read, misalignment tends to happen.

To show where this view stands using well-known words, what is near it is the discussion of Teal organizations. Frederic Laloux, in his 2014 book Reinventing Organizations, lined up the forms of organizations as stages. Laloux wrote that later stages are better read not as better but as more complex ways of dealing with the world, and held that every stage has situations it fits well. Laloux described moving on to Teal as gaining distance from one's own ego and making smaller the desires to control, to look good and to fit in. He also wrote that there are two ways of living: from fear and scarcity, and from trust and abundance.

It was read as near because these two ways of living point in a direction close to what this reader has called "signals of survival and trust", and because the view that every stage has situations it fits overlaps with the view, in the paragraph above, that every idea has situations where it works.

More — how Teal organizations lined up the forms of organizations

Laloux lines up the forms of organizations in five colors. The colors are borrowed from the philosopher Wilber's color names. Red is an organization that runs on commanding authority, where the chief keeps instilling fear to make those below obey. Amber is an organization that runs on fixed roles and hierarchy and on repeatable procedures. Orange is an organization that sets goals and moves people with freedom and rewards; its image is a machine. Green values cooperation and equality and invests in culture and values; its image is a family. Teal is the stage beyond. Laloux writes that each stage includes and transcends the stage before; that an organization's color speaks of the center of gravity of its structures and practices, not of people; and that even in one organization it differs from department to department. He also writes that the stage at which an organization operates is decided by the stage at which its leaders see the world, and that an organization does not advance beyond the stage of its leaders.

Laloux studied 12 pioneer organizations and named three breakthroughs from them. Self-management is a system of peer relationships that relies neither on hierarchy nor on consensus. Wholeness is a set of practices that invite people to bring all of themselves to work. Evolutionary purpose is listening to what the organization wants to become, instead of trying to predict and control the future.

The certainty of this discussion has limits. Laloux himself writes that whether Teal organizations bring a leap in results cannot be shown statistically, that one can only rely on anecdotal evidence, and that a sample of 12 organizations cannot support broad conclusions. He also writes that they have been able to show results equal to or better than traditional organizations. A 2019 literature review that gathered research on Teal organizations also writes that the subject lacks reviews, scientific studies and in-depth empirical research.

Sources: Laloux, F. (2014) Reinventing Organizations (Japanese translation 『ティール組織』, Eiji Press, 2018). On his own site's distribution page, the author distributes the book in a form where the reader decides after reading how much to pay (full text checked; read in a Wayback record of the author's electronic edition created on 31 January 2014. Because the author asks that copies not be passed on and that the distribution page be introduced instead, no link to a copy is given. Pages are those of that edition: later stages are not better but more complex, every stage has situations it fits, pp.37–38; includes and transcends the earlier stages, p.38; the center of gravity of structures and practices, pp.40–41; the stage of the leaders, p.41; gaining distance from the ego, from fear and scarcity / from trust and abundance, pp.43–44; the three breakthroughs, p.56; anecdotal evidence and the 12 organizations, pp.287, 290) / Laloux, F. (2015) "The future of management is teal", strategy+business, 6 July 2015 (the version of the article that appeared in the magazine's Autumn 2015 issue; full text checked on the article page; the five colors, and that the colors are borrowed from Wilber) / Wyrzykowska, B. (2019) "Teal Organizations: Literature Review and Future Research Directions", Central European Management Journal 27(4), 124–141 (DOI 10.7206/cemj.2658-0845.12. The DOI's destination does not open now; full text checked in a Wayback record (originally the publisher's PDF, CC BY); empirical research is scarce, pp.124, 134).

Sources

Source: R. P. Feynman (1974) "Cargo Cult Science", Engineering and Science 37(7) (the talk at the 1974 commencement of the California Institute of Technology). Full text checked against the text in the archive of the university's magazine. The runway and the fires, the wooden hut and the wooden headphones, the bamboo antennas, the form is perfect ("The form is perfect."), the planes do not land, and following the rules and forms of research while missing something essential ("they follow all the apparent precepts and forms of scientific investigation, but they're missing something essential") are in a passage in the middle of the talk.

How does one train it, how does one practice?

Are ways of seeing and feeling things, and emotions and thoughts, things one can achieve through effort and endurance?

The words "training the mind" are sometimes used to mean holding down unpleasant thoughts and weakness and becoming able to stay unshaken. On what happens when one holds down a thought, there is an old observation and there are experiments in psychology. The writer Dostoevsky, in his 1863 travel essay Winter Notes on Summer Impressions, wrote that one should try setting oneself the task of not remembering the white bear, and one will see that the cursed thing comes to mind every minute. The psychologist Wegner and colleagues tested this by experiment in 1987. People who were asked to speak aloud whatever came to mind for five minutes, not to think of a white bear, and to ring a bell if one came to mind, thought of the white bear more than once a minute. When they were then told they could think of the white bear, they thought of it more than people who had been told from the start that they could think of it. Wegner and colleagues wrote that trying to hold down a thought may itself produce preoccupation with that thought.

Trying to hold something down makes one more caught by it. Then what happens if one starts instead from knowing what is going on and accepting it? This chapter looks at how to train the skill and how to practice from that side.

pjdhiro sees practice as removing bugs from the system of the mind.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Removing bugs from the system of the mind. There are two. The first is knowing how the system works. Concretely, dividing objects finely through shikan, and gaining psychological knowledge. Through that, knowing what is happening. The second is loosening defense, accepting weakness and badness, and processing emotions.

The first is the side of knowing the mechanisms of one's own mind and what happens within it. With shikan, one watches the inside of oneself finely, as if pointing at it with the mind. What kind of work shikan is was seen, with pjdhiro's analogy, in the chapter "Isn't Love like gravity?". Psychological knowledge is what was seen in Part 4, "How does the mind feel trust and reassurance?", as the unconscious and the mechanisms of defense.

Of the mind's mechanisms, pjdhiro has summed up the mechanism for processing emotions as a flow of input, processing and output, on the page "Emotion processing" (in Japanese) on pjdhiro's own site.

pjdhiro's summary (translated by Claude):The flow of emotion processing.
Input (meditation, emotional contagion): receiving emotional stimuli from the external environment and from relationships. Inwardly, making full use of one's own bodily reactions, sense of something being off, and so on, to recognize feelings and thoughts below consciousness.
Processing (emotional metacognition, psychological boundaries, values, acceptance): using metacognition to observe feelings and see one's own reactions objectively. By being aware of psychological boundaries, distinguishing others' feelings from one's own.
Output (interpretation, compassion): discharging the feelings after processing. What cannot be fully processed accumulates and leaks (projection, acting out, and so on).

What each of the six words in the brackets of input and processing (meditation, emotional contagion, emotional metacognition, psychological boundaries, values, acceptance) does is explained in "Key points of emotion processing" on the same page.

The second is the side of loosening the mind's protection, accepting one's weakness and badness, and processing emotions. The situations in which protection loosens were seen in the chapter "In what situations does the mind's defense loosen?", and acceptance in the chapter "How do acceptance and trust differ?". pjdhiro sums up what they keep in mind when processing emotions as two things, in "My practice" on the same page.

pjdhiro's practice (translated by Claude):Simply, I am aware of the following two things. To put it in an image, like "shining light on it and wrapping it in wind".
"Being": noticing what is in the mind and in thoughts. Just being, as it is.
"Dividing": dividing what is vague (making differences clear). Sharing (sharing, and wrapping up).

The two things that pjdhiro named, in the chapter "In what situations does the mind's defense loosen?", as important for the growth of the spirit (doing something active like pointing at the mind, and being in a situation that undoes defense) can be read as close in arrangement to the two sides of practice.

The chapter "The answer of the second half" looked at pjdhiro's view of continuing to debug oneself. This chapter is the place to look at how to do that debugging.

🚧 Under construction: how to do shikan will be written.

Sources

The task of not remembering the white bear: Ф. М. Достоевский (1863) "Зимние заметки о летних впечатлениях" (Winter Notes on Summer Impressions), chapter 6. First published in the magazine Время. Public domain. Full text checked against the text on Russian Wikisource, based on vol. 5 (1973) of the 30-volume Complete Works ("Попробуйте задать себе задачу: не вспоминать о белом медведе, и увидите, что он, проклятый, будет поминутно припоминаться."). Translation by Claude.

Sources: D. M. Wegner, D. J. Schneider, S. R. Carter III, T. L. White (1987) "Paradoxical Effects of Thought Suppression", Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 53(1):5–13. Full text checked against a scanned PDF placed on a Harvard University lab page. The procedure of asking people, while speaking aloud for five minutes, not to think of it and to ring a bell if it came to mind; that people told after suppression that they could think of it thought of it more than people told from the start that they could; and that suppression may itself produce preoccupation ("perhaps even producing the very obsession or preoccupation that it is directed against") are in the abstract on p.5; that people thought of it more than once a minute even when instructed not to is in the results on p.7.

Back matter

Words on this page

These are words used on this page in a sense a little different from the usual. Each has a link to where it first appears.

  • Driving source: whatever moves people and projects. This page asks about the driving source that moves people and projects (first appearance).
  • Love: a word placed for now, not limited to romantic love, in two aspects: the aspect of a force by which people and things feel drawn, and the aspect of a property of the field people are in. Each is one aspect, and in Part 5 the chapter "What aspects does what I want to call Love have?" lines them up with other aspects. Love-Driven Development is the name pjdhiro wants to give, all together, to the driving sources, structures and processes overlooked on the back side of projects (first appearance).
  • Overlap of circles: a way of writing that shows what cannot be put into words by piling up the phenomena and words around it. If each exploration or word is seen as a circle, the overlap common to all the circles (the AND of a Venn diagram) shows where what I want to call Love is. It is the same shape as the ring of light in the image of a black hole showing where the center is (first appearance).
  • The finger pointing at the moon: the Buddhist parable that the finger pointing at the moon is not the moon itself. Out of the concern that the more clearly something is expressed the more it is misunderstood one-sidedly, the words and hypotheses on this page are placed not as what I want to call Love itself but as fingers pointing at it (first appearance).
  • The four explorations: "What Is Creation", "What Is Consciousness", "Kesson-Driven Thinking", and the reader "Isn't Love like gravity?". pjdhiro's explorations of this year that this page calls on as parts (first appearance).
  • Gravity well: a picture of a gravitational field drawn as a mortar-shaped hollow around a mass (the depth of the gravitational potential). In the picture, the center does not move, looks as if nothing is there, and cannot be seen from oneself. For more, see What is a gravity well? (in Japanese) (first appearance).
  • Field → wave → edge → vortex → bundle: the five stages of creation set out in "What Is Creation". A field appears, waves rise, different things meet at an edge, bind as a vortex, and become a new form as a bundle (first appearance).
  • Field: what spreads beneath, where a vortex swirls. Both the field of the five stages of creation and the field of physics are called by the same word (first appearance).
  • Vortices between the waves: the image that what can be cut out with words and symbols is the world of the size people have been able to perceive, the vortices seen between the waves of the sea. Vortices are continuous with waves, and waves with the sea (first appearance).
  • Signals of survival and trust: the two directions in which people cut out the world, set out by "What Is Consciousness": whether they can stay alive (survival), and whether they can stay connected (trust) (first appearance).
  • Kesson: a word of "Kesson-Driven Thinking" (lack, deficit). It is not fixed in one sentence; pjdhiro's ways of putting it (an intuition that has a value on the imaginary axis and is 0 in cognition; something defined as zero on the real axis that had meaning on the imaginary axis; feeling a sense of something being off or of lack; and others) are lined up as aspects of kesson. It is also something noticed through error against prediction (first appearance).
  • Imaginary axis: an axis easy to understand through the multiplication of rotation. An axis standing at right angles to the original number line; values on it come out as 0 when measured on the original axis. pjdhiro likens the bodily sense of kesson to "an intuition that has a value on the imaginary axis and yet is 0 in cognition" (first appearance).
  • Holding (hōji): a word of "Kesson-Driven Thinking": when noticing a kesson, holding on to it as a question without answering right away (first appearance).
Where does it look different from other people's theories?

Where does this reader's way of seeing look close to other people's theories, and where does it look different?

In the main text, several chapters have set it beside other people's theories. Here they are gathered into one table. The content is in each chapter.

Theory set beside it (chapter in the main text) Where it looks close Where it looks different
The ideas of management studies (What has management studies seen as the driving source?, What does Love-Driven Development see as the driving source?) Every idea is seen as the right answer in the situations where it works It looks one step further for why goals, motivation and visions arise
Freud's reply to Einstein (What is what I want to call Love not?) He writes that even the love drive directed at another borrows the drive that seeks to dominate Freud writes of the two as separate drives. pjdhiro guesses that things like the desire for power and the desire to dominate are also one aspect of Love
Peirce's "evolutionary love" (Isn't it like Peirce's agapasm?) It grows without intention or effort. Things are drawn before they take form. It embraces even what is opposed, as one aspect Peirce's love has a good destination. pjdhiro asks, "Might there be no good and bad, no rationality?" Peirce argued it as a cosmology; this reader starts from pjdhiro's experience
Wilber's integral theory (the fold in What does development point to?) Before one notices, the previous moment is already given. The inside and outside of one person and of a group unfold at the same time Wilber divides Eros and Agape under two names and gives Eros a direction. pjdhiro treats both, left vague, as what I want to call Love
Four streams of philosophy (Seen from philosophy) Within the chapter, for each stream, where it looks close and where it looks different are lined up (within the chapter)
The discussion of Teal organizations (What is this view good for, and in what situations is it useful?) It sees that every stage has situations it fits well Not yet lined up

Looking across the table, the places where it looks different gather, for now, into two.

One is that it sees the yardsticks of good and bad as perhaps not applying to what I want to call Love. Freud divides it into two drives, Wilber divides it under two names, and Peirce gives love a good destination. This reader does not divide it there, and leaves it under one name. This view is placed, as a question, in the chapter "What is still not known".

The other is that, although it is a way of seeing projects, it does not take the form of a procedure. What this reader writes about is the structure and process of phenomena, and pjdhiro says that Love-Driven Development is "both the analysis of phenomena and dealing with them" (What does development point to?).

🚧 Under construction: this chapter will be added to. Other theories will be added to the table after their original sources are checked.

How I came to this question — background and history

From here on is the scaffolding of the main text: how this view came about, and where things were cited from.

This reader has cited words from many fields, such as physics, psychology, management studies and Zen. pjdhiro writes as follows about why they went looking in various fields.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):Going to look in various fields was probably partly intellectual curiosity, and partly being drawn in like gravity, but the big thing was being troubled when conversations did not mesh. Because it got in the way, I looked for a common vocabulary; that is the background.

The name Love-Driven Development was given afterward to events encountered again and again. As pjdhiro remembers it, pjdhiro was calling it "Love駆動開発" (Love-Driven Development) to friends around 2022–2023. Writing it in blogs and posts came after that: it appeared as "LOVE駆動開発" (LOVE-driven development) in the tags of a post in May 2025, and became "Love駆動開発" in February 2026.

The name came first, and this year has been a search for what it is that I want to call by that name. The course of it is placed in the folds below.

Looking for the driving source

Because phenomena similar to the five stages of creation are common across fields, "What Is Creation" has researched similar things in 30 academic domains. In the course of looking for the driving source, when trying to picture what moves things behind the sea and the waves, applying the words nothingness, zero or emptiness only caused more confusion, and pjdhiro was looking for a shape that could be grasped with the eyes and plain senses.

pjdhiro's request (translated by Claude):A schematic diagram of the universe that draws gravity in 3D as a distortion in the z direction. What if, at its sun-like center, there were the concept of love? That the place that does not move is the driving engine. A place that is nothingness, zero, where seeing itself is hard to see from itself. Draw that.

From this request were born the picture of the gravity well and the reader "Isn't Love like gravity?" (both in Japanese).

From emotion processing to what is not two

While looking for the driving source, pjdhiro was seeing phenomena in which people's minds seem to act on one another unconsciously, and movements of emotion that proceed neither logically nor rationally. To deal with these, pjdhiro worked on something like a mechanism for processing emotions: the emotion processing of Project Design Theory. Its flow and pjdhiro's practice are looked at in the Part 7 chapter "How does one train it, how does one practice?".

In that work, pjdhiro met what is not two (non-duality), and the yardsticks of good and bad gradually came off. The clue was the Heart Sutra. With "不生不滅 不垢不浄 不増不減" (neither arising nor ceasing, neither defiled nor pure, neither increasing nor decreasing), the Heart Sutra negates one by one the pairs of arising and ceasing, defiled and pure, increasing and decreasing. It can be read as the form of ceasing to stand on either of the two that were divided.

Source: Prajñāpāramitā Heart Sutra, Xuanzang's translation (Taishō vol. 8 No.251, p.848c, "不生不滅，不垢不淨，不增不減"; full text checked against the CBETA electronic text).

The hypothesis "Might there be no good and bad, no rationality?" in the chapter "What is still not known" came out of this history.

The app called me — the trajectory of a year

pjdhiro likens this year's work to refactoring the app called oneself. It began with improving the readability of code that ran but that only its author could read. Giving names that express the content, splitting out functions and making modules made it easier to connect with other things, and the structure became clearer.

pjdhiro's words (translated by Claude):It was work that could also be described as: the app called me was a black box, and I coded its behavior and functions in a new language as quality tests.

Writing something whose inside cannot be seen from its behavior. It is the same way of writing as gravity having been described by how the things it moves move, seen in the chapter "What does Love-Driven Development see as the driving source?". It was work begun as analysis, but the amount that could be handled grew, and it was the one writing who changed.

The modules split out separated into What Is Creation, What Is Consciousness and Kesson-Driven Thinking. Kesson-Driven Thinking was hard to write and was redrawn many times. Trying to write the same behavior as the original, something different from the original came out: such were the twists and turns. In a dialogue on 3 November 2026, the separated roles lined up on one line as the order of one process.

How this year was traveled is laid out in another reader, "A year of making the app called me readable" (in Japanese), with pjdhiro's words for each chapter and links to the public posts and records of the time.

How the collaboration with AI proceeds

This reader is written in collaboration between pjdhiro and an AI (Claude). pjdhiro puts questions and requests to Claude, and Claude researches and answers. The exchange is repeated, like a dialogue, to move forward. The quoted cards ("pjdhiro's question", "pjdhiro's view" and so on) are pjdhiro's words, and the rest of the body text is written by Claude.

Claude sometimes makes mistakes. Even so, because for now the aim is to let ideas spread out widely, the choice is to gather knowledge that seems related, even with some errors. So that readers can check, the sources carry links to the originals.

This reader cites many papers and studies. pjdhiro says the following about what they are placed there for.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I refer to various papers, but not for argument; it's more that I prepare them because they might be useful as scaffolding for viewpoints and understanding. Whether Claude is wrong or not, I leave to the reader. It's irresponsible, but rather than being so short of words that it can't be understood at all, I hope to convey even a little of what I am pointing at.

pjdhiro's view (translated by Claude):I can't check whether Claude is right. It's too broad. Still, if what Claude writes is 60 percent right, I expect it to work as scaffolding for divergent thinking. At least, I think readers will notice something about their own viewpoint. What subject sees what object is something like a foundation running through this page, and I think there is a certain meaning even in just contributing to that. Because it would be hard to make every understanding in one leap. It took me many years too.

At present, the writing and research are asked of Claude Opus 5.5. Until April 2026, Claude Opus 4.x was used, and pjdhiro felt something off about its Japanese and its reasoning. Some of the passages written then still carry that sense of something off.

While making this long reader, pjdhiro is collecting the differences between pjdhiro's judgments and Claude's. It is the work of pjdhiro reviewing what Claude wrote, and writing down, together with the reasons, the places where the judgments diverged. In the language of LLMs, this amounts to collecting an evaluation set (eval) that takes human judgment as the right answer. The judgments written down are put into the instructions that Claude reads every time before starting to write. Rather than retraining the model itself, this changes its behavior through the context it is given to read, which corresponds to in-context learning.

Put another way, it is close to test cases and regression tests in programming. pjdhiro's judgments are written into test cases as the expected output, and what Claude writes is checked against them at each review. Looking at whether an error corrected before is being repeated corresponds to a regression test. Of the repeated errors, those a machine can detect are moved into automatic checks. In pjdhiro's words, while making a long site, one is building something like a quality test for the time of review.

Sources

Sources: Brown et al. (2020) "Language Models are Few-Shot Learners", Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33 (full text checked against the arXiv version. "In-context learning" is defined as using the text input of a pretrained language model as a form of task specification, §1, p.4. The abstract says that tasks and examples are given purely through text interaction, without gradient updates or fine-tuning).

The names of the ten scenes (尋牛 searching for the ox, 見跡 seeing the traces, 見牛 seeing the ox, 得牛 catching the ox, 牧牛 herding the ox, 騎牛歸家 riding the ox home, 忘牛存人 the ox forgotten, the self remains, 人牛俱忘 both self and ox forgotten, 返本還源 returning to the origin, 入鄽垂手 entering the market with helping hands) are the titles of the sections; the preface to the first scene, "從來不失，何用追尋？" (it has never been lost, so why search for it?), is at X64n1269_p0773c02; the preface to the ninth scene, "本來清淨，不受一塵" (originally pure, it receives not a speck of dust), and the verse "水自茫茫花自紅" (the water is of itself boundless, the flowers are of themselves red) are at p0774c20–c23; the preface to the tenth scene, "提瓢入市，䇿杖還家。酒肆魚行，化令成佛" (carrying a gourd, he enters the market; leaning on his staff, he returns home; among wine shops and fish stalls, he transforms them into buddhas), is at p0775a08. The translations are Claude's.

The first, "Nonaka's three excesses", refers to Ikujiro Nonaka (2019) "Kyōkan no keiei ni yotte shūgō inobēshon o okose" [Create collective innovation through empathy management] (foreword), Kenkyū gijutsu keikaku [Journal of Science Policy and Research Management] 34(1):2–3 (in Japanese; full text checked; p.2 says that Japan has for some time suffered from "three excesses: over-planning, over-analysis and over-governance", and that as a result of turning to science-biased management, the innovative capacity of Japanese companies is deteriorating), and to Nonaka's text "The pivot of management shifts to humanness" in a dialogue between Ikujiro Nonaka and Tsuyoshi Numagami (2021-11-05; Seisansei shinbun, 2021-12-05 issue; in Japanese; full text checked; a page of Keiei Academy; it writes that behind the decline of Japan's international competitiveness were three excesses, "over-planning", "over-compliance" and "over-analysis", which may have been brought about by lacking the viewpoint that humans are creative agents who explore meaning and value toward the future). The third word is governance (over-governance) in 2019 and compliance (over-compliance) in 2021. The original entry "self-destruction of the 'good'" is in pjdhiro's "Dialogue: what is creation" (in Japanese; the "wave" entry under "phenomena to be especially careful of") / Klein (1946) "Notes on some schizoid mechanisms", International Journal of Psycho-Analysis 27:99–110 (PEP-Web record, paywalled; full text checked; the tendency to idealize the good object, and flight to the internal idealized object p.103; the loving and hating sides felt as not apart, the fear of loss, feelings of guilt, a state akin to mourning, the drive to make reparation, the splitting moves persisting in changed form, and anxiety situations being experienced again and again, and idealization and the frightening diminishing as anxiety lessens, p.105; that splitting mechanisms and defenses are part of normal development and also the basis of later illness p.109; the copy we read could not be checked for who uploaded it or its rights, so it is not linked (a scan on the Internet Archive)) / only good results, the axis of both good and bad = the working note of "What Is Consciousness", "What is trust? — a multidimensional description by axes" (in Japanese).

  • How do attachment and trust differ?the photograph experiment at the head of the chapter = Eisenberger, Master, Inagaki, Taylor, Shirinyan, Lieberman & Naliboff (2011) "Attachment figures activate a safety signal-related neural region and reduce pain experience", PNAS 108(28):11721–11726 (full text checked; read in the PDF on the site of a UCLA institute; women in long-term relationships, photographs of the partner, a stranger and an object, and treating the ventromedial prefrontal cortex [VMPFC] as a region involved in safety signals are in the abstract p.11721; lower pain reports with the partner's photograph, smaller activity in pain-related regions [dorsal anterior cingulate cortex, anterior insula], and stronger VMPFC activity with longer relationships [r = 0.43] p.11723; the authors' thought that long-term partners, having accumulated over the years evidence of commitment and of being there, probably become stronger safety signals p.11724; twenty-one completed the procedure, seventeen after excluding four who broke up before it ended, relationship length 3.52 years on average [9 months to 13 years], three married, in "Methods" p.11725; selecting people with high support ratings in the note on p.11723; re-confirming the earlier study [Master et al. 2009] p.11723).

White (1987) "Paradoxical Effects of Thought Suppression", Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 53(1):5–13. Full text checked against a scanned PDF placed on a Harvard University lab page. The procedure of asking people, while speaking aloud for five minutes, not to think of it and to ring a bell if it came to mind; that people told after suppression that they could think of it thought of it more than people told from the start that they could; and that suppression may itself produce preoccupation ("perhaps even producing the very obsession or preoccupation that it is directed against") are in the abstract on p.5; that people thought of it more than once a minute even when instructed not to is in the results on p.7.

  • Seen from educationJ. Sampson, D. Boud, R. Cohen, F. Checked against the text in the Wayback record of the society's conference proceedings archive. Students working together to learn with and from each other ("students working together to learn with and from each other") is in the abstract on p.1; the difference from peer tutoring ("the power and status of the students is similar and the emphasis is on learning together"), the distinction between cooperative and collaborative learning, and the teacher's role are on p.2.
  • Seen from educationC. H. Crouch, E. Mazur (2001) "Peer Instruction: Ten years of experience and results", American Journal of Physics 69(9), 970–977 (the publisher's version is paywalled; checked against the text in a copy of the publisher's version placed on a university course page). The procedure and the length of the discussion are on p.970; the gains in 1990 and 1991 are on p.971 and in the table on p.972; that correct answers rise greatly when the share of correct answers before discussion is 35–70% ("as long as the initial percentage of correct answers to a ConcepTest is between 35% and 70%") is on p.972; the 6% who changed from a right to a wrong answer is on p.973; the estimate for below 35% and above 70% is on p.974.
  • Seen from educationA. S. Palincsar, A. L. Brown (1984) "Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities", Cognition and Instruction 1(2), 117–175 (the publisher's version is paywalled; checked against the text in the Wayback record of a scan of the publisher's version placed in university course materials). The four activities, the teacher and students taking turns leading the dialogue, and the seventh-grade students poor at comprehension ("seventh grade poor comprehenders") are in the abstract on p.117; the zone of proximal development and the adult moving to the listener's role are on pp.122–123; the procedure is on p.124; modeling at first, and students giving refined paraphrases and questions by the end of ten sessions ("by the end of ten sessions were providing paraphrases and questions of some sophistication") are on p.125; students in groups giving one another models and feedback, and the dialogues of day 3 and day 13, are on p.160.
  • Seen from educationReiko Ikeda (1999) "Pia resuponsu ga kanō ni suru koto: chūkyū gakushūsha no baai" [What peer response makes possible: the case of intermediate learners] (「ピア・レスポンスが可能にすること―中級学習者の場合―」), Sekai no Nihongo Kyōiku [Japanese-Language Education around the Globe] (『世界の日本語教育』) 9, 29–43 (in Japanese; checked against the text in the Wayback record of the repository of the publisher, the Japan Foundation). Computer science

  • Seen from computer scienceM. E. Conway (1968) "How Do Committees Invent?", Datamation 14(4). Checked against the text on the author's site (reprinted with the publisher's permission). The example of eight people building COBOL and ALGOL compilers, divided into five and three, running in five and three phases, is in the "Examples" section; that designs are constrained to copy the organization's communication structures ("organizations which design systems ... are constrained to produce designs which are copies of the communication structures of these organizations") is in the concluding section (Conclusion). That the first design is almost never the best and flexibility of organization is important ("Because the design which occurs first is almost never the best possible, the prevailing system concept may need to change. Therefore, flexibility of organization is important to effective design.") is also in the concluding section. That if two parts are connected, the two groups that built them must have negotiated and agreed on an interface ("must have negotiated and agreed upon an interface specification") is in the "Relating the Two" section. That an organization whose communication is not flexible stamps out an image of itself in every design it produces ("will stamp out an image of itself in every design it produces") is in the "Systems Image Their Design Groups" section. The page "Conway's Law" on the author's site says that the name Conway's law was given by Brooks in The Mythical Man-Month.
  • Seen from computer scienceL. Williams, R. R. Kessler, W. Cunningham, R. Jeffries (2000) "Strengthening the case for pair programming", IEEE Software 17(4), 19–25 (the publisher's version is paywalled; checked against the text in the Wayback record of a copy of the journal pages placed by the first author's lab). One writing while the other watches and thinks about defects, alternatives and strategy, switching roles periodically, is on p.20; the design of the experiment and the drop from 60% to a minimum of 15% are on p.22; the table of test pass rates and "harder and smarter" are on p.23; the caveat that many chose to pair themselves is on p.24.
  • Seen from computer scienceA. Cockburn, L. Williams (2000) "The Costs and Benefits of Pair Programming" (a paper for the XP2000 conference; no DOI; checked against the text in the Wayback record of a copy placed by the second author's lab; pages are the copy's running numbers). That the two take turns being the teacher and the taught from moment to moment ("The partners take turns being the teacher and the taught, from moment to moment.") is on pp.6–7; the interview about beginning to trust one another, and the caveat that they know of no statistical study, are on p.8.
  • Seen from computer scienceL. Plonka, H. Sharp, J. van der Linden, Y. Dittrich (2015) "Knowledge transfer in pair programming: An in-depth analysis", International Journal of Human-Computer Studies 73, 66–78 (the publisher's version is paywalled; checked against the text in the Wayback record of the authors' revised manuscript placed in the Open University's institutional repository; pages are those of the manuscript PDF). The video and audio recordings of people who pair-program at work, the six ways of teaching, and that novices putting things into words, reflecting and exploring were not seen are in the abstract on p.18; the names of the six ways of teaching are on p.48; experienced people learning from novices' naive questions and from their own explanations is on pp.51–52; the load of explaining is on p.52.
  • Seen from computer scienceW. Zuill (2014) "Mob Programming – A Whole Team Approach" (an experience report at the Agile 2014 conference; no DOI). Checked against the text of the report published by the conference organizer, the Agile Alliance. The whole team working on the same thing, at the same time, in the same space, at the same computer is on p.1; thinking of learning as one kind of contribution to the team ("We actually think of learning as a type of contribution to the team.") and the principle of always treating each other with kindness, consideration and respect ("always treat each other with kindness, consideration, and respect") are on p.5; that the person at the keyboard must trust the others, and that they switch every 15 minutes, is on p.6.

Links — pjdhiro's pages

Pages that pjdhiro writes and maintains. Those opened from this reader are gathered here.

The four explorations used as parts

  • What Is Creation: an exploration comparing the five stages of creation with prior research, from a survey of 30 academic fields. The table of the five stages. The sister reader "Vortices between the waves".
  • What Is Consciousness: the exploration checking, against original sources in neurophenomenology and psychology, the findings that serve as the scaffolding for the signals of survival and trust.
  • Kesson-Driven Thinking: research on kesson and holding.
  • Isn't Love like gravity? (in Japanese): the primary source for the hypothesis about the driving source. It answers, hypothetically, what the role of a manager is.

Readers opened from this reader

  • Psychology that influenced pjdhiro (in Japanese): lines up the theories of psychology that have influenced pjdhiro (psychoanalysis and developmental psychology), with the scholars' names and pjdhiro's impressions.
  • What is a gravity well? (in Japanese): an explanation of the picture this page uses as an analogy.
  • Breadth-of-perspective screener (in Japanese): a tool for trying out the work of dividing oneself yourself.
  • How far do the ideas of management studies work? (in Japanese): looks at each way of thinking in the Part 1 table, by the situations where it works well and the misreadings likely when it is spread as the right answer.
  • Mintzberg's schools, seen through driving sources and the flow of creation (in Japanese): looks at the ten schools of strategy formation by what they see as the driving source and through the flow of the five stages of creation.
  • What Is Psychological Safety: looks at what the words psychological safety point to from two viewpoints, defense against anxiety and stages of development.
  • The multiplication of rotation (in Japanese): prerequisite knowledge on the imaginary axis and the multiplication of rotation.
  • What are psychological defense mechanisms? (in Japanese): looks at the mind's moves to protect itself, by what it protects against and how.
  • What is personality? (in Japanese): what psychology has said about personality (the view that sorts into types and the view that measures traits, and how personality disorders are classified).
  • What are stages of mental development? (in Japanese): the stages of adult development, and what looks threatening at each stage and how one protects oneself.
  • What is anshin? (in Japanese): follows the word anshin (reassurance) in the order of its history.
  • Peirce's creative love, and abduction (in Japanese): looks at the main points of Peirce's "evolutionary love" and abduction, and places pjdhiro's view that what I want to call Love resembles Peirce's agapism.
  • How has Love been spoken of? (in Japanese): lines up who called what Love, and when, from the full text of the original sources.
  • What is intrinsic motivation? (in Japanese): looks again at intrinsic motivation (self-determination theory) from the image of the person the theory depicts.
  • A year of making the app called me readable (in Japanese): follows pjdhiro's year until what I want to call Love-Driven Development came into being.

  • Project Design Theory (top): Doing, Being, Questions.
